6 free and inexpensive sites teaching students to code
2026-09-23

6 free and inexpensive sites teaching students to code

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
If you want to learn how to code, but in your own time and without it costing an arm and a leg, here are some useful resources. In 2026, despite the rising popularity of vibe coding, there are a multitude of STEM-based careers that require employees to have strong, traditional coding skills. You may want to improve yours or even begin from scratch, but if you find that standard courses are costly or too time consuming, it can be a deterrent. But fret not, because there are plenty of opportunities to develop coding, skills be it with personal or professional goals in mind – and they don’t have to drain your time or your finances. So, if you want to build on your programming skills, but aren’t sure where to go, the following six resources could be exactly what you are looking for. Codewars is a site for the students and professionals who want to broaden their skills, but who also want to have a little fun along the way. The platform aims to help learners “achieve mastery through challenge” via training with peers on code kata – small coding exercises – that continuously challenges. Users can sharpen their skills, go up in the ranks though the challenge board, engage with a broad community of fellow learners and receive instant feedback to determine if progress is being made. You can even create your own challenges and questions and put them to the wider community. The creators aptly refer to the site as a coding dojo and whether you are a student or sensei, there is definitely something to be gleaned from Codewars. UK-based Exercism describes itself as a platform focused on “building a place where anyone can learn and master programming for free, without ever feeling lost or stupid”. Students of all abilities can learn how to code in 83 different languages via articles, videos and project work, with the goal being to have fun and progress, without stress or competition. The platform also offers a wide community of peers with which to collaborate and build friendships, as well as a wide network of mentors to guide the learning process. FreeCodeCamp is exactly what it sounds like. This free platform is a resource through which students can learn how to code via a collection of podcasts, videos, articles and interactive lessons. Putting the closet in the cloud is a complex challenge, one that attracts people with an entrepreneurial spirit. The most actionable data to drive superior results The available curriculum is expansive, can be engaged with via a number of different spoken languages and comes with a free certificate of completion. There are available learning options for beginners all the way through to advanced students and engagement is solely at the discretion of the user. If you are a professional or a recent graduate preparing for a job interview that will likely demand robust coding skills, LeetCode is the ideal resource to test yourself in advance. The free-to-use platform supports 14 popular coding languages and enables users to debug and write their own online projects. Students can test their coding knowledge by answering some of the more than 4,300 questions available. Users can also engage with a wide community of peers, participate in contests, challenge themselves and earn rewards. If you have some skills but are looking to hone them, with future career opportunities in mind, this is definitely the place for you. For those who want a broader experience, there is also a premium paid feature. The Odin Project, which is a free open-source learning platform, is an ideal site for a student or professional looking to advance their coding skills. The goal is to empower students to embark upon a career that requires coding, particularly those looking to become developers, though it is open to anyone who wishes to use it. The platform offers a hands-on introduction to all of the essential tools you’ll need to build real, working websites and also offers information on what web developers actually do and the foundations they might need for advancing their education. “This project is designed to fill in the gap for people who are trying to hack it on their own but still want a high-quality education,” according to the site. Programiz’s mission is to create a space in which learners can learn how to program easily and at their own pace. The platform offers tutorials in a variety of languages, the browser is free to use and no installation is required. Programiz claims to teach the skill of coding step by step, via the use of online compilers available in more than 20 languages and interactive lessons that offer a guided path to learning programming with practical code that runs in your browser. Research Ireland funding creates eight spin-outs, report shows

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
From left: Dr Ruth Freeman; Dr Diarmuid O’Brien; Minister James Lawless, TD; Dr Siobhán Roche; and Michael Horgan. Image: Kenneth O’Halloran In its first annual report, the agency says it paid out €313m in research grants to researchers in 2025. The Irish Government’s science and innovation agency, Research Ireland, committed €288m to more than 1,000 research awards last year. The organisation was formally created in August 2024 after Science Foundation Ireland was merged with the Irish Research Council. In its first annual report under its new persona, Research Ireland reports that it manages €610m in committed funding and paid out €313m in grants to researchers in 2025. That investment leveraged a further €310m, which includes €129m from the EU, nearly €77m in private funding, €24m from Enterprise Ireland and €25m from other Government funding sources. Non-Exchequer funding rose to €227.3m, up 14pc since 2024. According to the 2025 report published today (23 September), the agency gave out nearly €290m in 1,053 new awards across its range of programmes. Its funding supported more than 3,000 PhDs and 1,000 investigators, and nearly 1,500 postdoctoral fellows. 40pc of its funded principal investigators and co-principal investigators are women, along with 51pc of grant holders, it reports. Collectively, the researchers supported by the agency’s funding delivered nearly 6,000 publications, 167 invention disclosures and launched eight new spin-outs. Speaking at the annual report’s launch, Minister for Further and Higher Education, Research, Innovation and Science James Lawless, TD said that the report provided an opportunity to reflect on the impact of public investment in research. “It also comes at a time of continued momentum, with Research Ireland advancing key initiatives including the new Rinn network, Global Talent Ireland and working in collaboration with the Higher Education Authority to deliver Inspire, helping to ensure Ireland can deliver and support world-class research and innovation.” Michael Horgan, Research Ireland’s chairperson added: “The creation of Research Ireland marked an important step in strengthening Ireland’s research and innovation system. “Since its establishment, the agency has successfully brought together the strengths and expertise of the organisations that formed Research Ireland, while maintaining a commitment to excellence, transparency and impact.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. The Interview: Digital euro will offer ‘cash-like privacy’, says Dombrovskis
2026-09-23

The Interview: Digital euro will offer ‘cash-like privacy’, says Dombrovskis

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
EU commissioner Valdis Dombrovskis addresses a Research Ireland gathering on 17 September. Image: Finbarr O’Rourke We sat down with Commissioner Valdis Dombrovskis last week. He told us the digital euro would offer ‘cash-like privacy’ and cut Europe’s reliance on foreign card schemes. The digital euro could go live in 2029 if the Irish Presidency of the Council of the EU gets the legislation over the line before the end of the year, according to EU commissioner Valdis Dombrovskis, formerly prime minister of Latvia. Speaking to SiliconRepublic.com at a visit to Research Ireland last week (17 September) Dombrovskis, the European commissioner for economy and productivity, implementation and simplification, said Brussels was firmly behind Ireland’s push to conclude talks this year. “The Irish Presidency has an ambition to finalise the legislative process this year, and from the Commission side, we are very much supporting this,” he said. “And if this is the case, then ECB [European Central Bank] can start the preparations in earnest, and then it would go live as of 2029.” Ireland took over the rotating Presidency on 1 July and holds it until 31 December, putting it in the chair for the final stage of negotiations between the Council, the European Parliament and the Commission, known as trilogues. The first trilogue took place on 13 July and a further round was scheduled for late September. The Irish Presidency’s programme commits it to working towards agreement on the digital euro framework by year-end. It has been a long road. The Commission first tabled its proposal in June 2023. The Council agreed its negotiating mandate in December 2025 and the European Parliament adopted its position in July. Much of the public debate has centred on privacy and fears that a digital currency issued by a central bank could be used to monitor how people spend. Dombrovskis said those concerns had been addressed. “We have strong privacy safeguards in place, in the offline functionality of the digital euro. It actually offers almost cash-like privacy,” he said. Indeed, he argued that the digital euro would reveal less about people’s spending than the payment cards they use now. “In a sense, the central bank will know less about citizens’ transactions with the digital euro than banks know now from card transactions.” He was also keen to stress that the digital euro is not intended to replace notes and coins. “We had been very clear that digital euro is there to complement cash, not to replace cash. It’s a way to bring central bank money into the 21st century, in the digital era.” Alongside the digital euro proposal, the Commission put forward a separate measure to strengthen the legal tender status of cash. Dombrovskis said this would mean “obliging [euro] area member states to ensure sufficient availability and acceptance of cash so that it can fulfil its legal tender functions”. For Dombrovskis, the case for the digital euro rests on two points. The first is that cash, the only form of central bank money currently available to the public, is in decline. “Currently, central bank money is cash. And cash use is declining. So that’s why digital euro is a way to bring central bank money in to the digital era and to provide it as an important monetary anchor,” he said. The second is Europe’s dependence on payment networks based outside the bloc. “It is a way to develop European-based payment solutions because currently we are heavily relying on card schemes which are foreign-based.” He added: “With this digital euro project, the [European] Central Bank will also develop a pan-euro area payment solution and infrastructure, which we currently don’t have.” It is all part of the current push towards sovereignty, Dombrovskis said. “This is also a matter of strategic autonomy of the euro area in this more conflictual and less stable world. Financial stability is the blood flow of the economy, so it’s important in a sense that we have our own structure and are not dependent on outside players.” The digital euro sits within Dombrovskis’s wider push on EU competitiveness. When we spoke, he acknowledged that productivity growth in Europe has trailed the US and China for at least two decades, and agreed there was no quick fix. “There’s probably not one single bullet we can say, you know, we need to do this and that fixes our [competitiveness] problem,” he said. But he argued Europe has more going for it than it often admits. “We also obviously should not talk ourselves down. We have many strengths. We have the largest single market of 450m consumers.” Dombrovskis was visiting Research Ireland as part of his Irish visit, and met several prize-winning teams supported through the National Challenge Fund, which is funded under Ireland’s National Recovery and Resilience Plan. Addressing the gathering, he said the research and innovation undertaken here in Ireland continued to make a vital contribution to Europe’s overall competitiveness, resilience and prosperity today. “It is a contribution that is needed more than ever,” he said. “We are living in a fast-changing and increasingly fragmented world.  Our prosperity and security depends on generating knowledge, turning it into new technologies and bringing those innovations to market.” He noted that Ireland recently marked 60 years since the decision to introduce free secondary school education in Ireland. “It is credited with transforming the prospects of the Irish economy and laying the foundations for the prosperity the country continues to enjoy today. We should draw inspiration from such a far-sighted, visionary policy to help reach our common objective today: to ensure that Europe remains a global leader in science, technology and innovation.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Amethyst bags €100,000 for its AI voice assistant for the elderly

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
The tool sends reminders and checks in with older people to help prolong their independence at home. Dublin-based health-tech start-up Amethyst Care has bagged €100,000 from Enterprise Ireland’s (EI) dedicated fund for pre-seed start-ups. Amethyst offers an AI voice assistant specifically designed for elderly people. Developed at EI and Trinity College Dublin’s Adapt centre by Rebecca McManus, the voice tool gives medication reminders and does wellness check-ins to help older people remain independent for longer. It also helps clinicians collect data on an ongoing basis to help monitor patients’ health in the hopes of catching and resolving issues before a trip to the hospital becomes necessary, the company said. Amethyst said it wants to address loneliness and isolation – key contributors to hospital re-admissions – while also supporting healthier ageing at home. The fresh funding comes after the company launched an office in Ohio, US. EI’s Pre-Seed Start Fund offers €50,000 or €100,000 one-time investments to early-stage companies with viable products, a well-identified customer base and a plan to scale. Funding comes alongside 10 sessions with the agency’s panel of mentors. Last year, EI invested more than €9.5m in pre-seed funding across more than 100 start-ups. “Problems older people living alone face include loneliness, remembering to take medications on time and cognitive decline,” McManus told SiliconRepublic.com in a 2024 interview. Her solution, she said, is a cheaper alternative to private home care. McManus has more than a decade of experience in sales across companies such as FlowForma, Citrix and Pluralsight. The 2024-founded Amethyst is also advised by Adapt’s co-founder and former CEO Vincent Wade. “Our goal is to delay the time in which an older person suffering with mobility-related issues needs to go into full-time care,” McManus said in 2024. “Along with improving delays in the healthcare system, we hope to use technology to coincide with face-to-face care, to keep vulnerable people at home for longer.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. ShinyHunters says it breached the FBI and stole employee data
2026-09-23

ShinyHunters says it breached the FBI and stole employee data

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
The data breach is in retaliation for alleged disinformation published by the FBI about ShinyHunters’ cybercrime methods, according to the group. Cybercriminal group ShinyHunters said it breached the US Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI) yesterday (22 September) and stole the personal information of a number of current and former FBI employees. A screenshot shared by the group with a number of outlets reportedly showed information ShinyHunters had obtained on 5,000 FBI agents, including names, addresses, phone numbers and details on the employees’ spouses. Reuters said it could not verify whether the screenshot was real, but was able to partially verify the authenticity of some of the information displayed – the outlet was able to match details from the leak to FBI employees in at least 10 instances, one of which was FBI director Kash Patel. However, the publication said it could not establish where the data came from, or whether it had been stolen from the FBI’s internal systems as claimed by ShinyHunters. According to 404 Media, which cited an FBI spokesperson, ShinyHunters was able to instigate the breach through a zero-day exploit in an Oracle product called PeopleSoft, after which the group managed to access AWS GovCloud servers and download data. The total exfiltrated data amounts to between 2TB and 3TB, according to the publication. In a statement first posted to the group’s website on the dark web, ShinyHunters said it had targeted the FBI due to a report the agency published about the group earlier this year, which detailed the group’s methods and advised people not to pay if targeted for ransom. ShinyHunters said the FBI made false allegations about the group and its cybercrime methods in the report – including alleged usage of threatening phone calls and false claims of compromising information – and has given the FBI one week to “correct or simply remove” the report. It’s unclear what the group plans to do with the data if the one-week deadline passes. When asked by BleepingComputer if it would release the stolen data if the FBI failed to correct or remove the report, ShinyHunters said: “No comment.” Commenting on the FBI breach, Closed Door Security CEO William Wright told SiliconRepublic.com: “Hell hath no fury like a ShinyHunter scorned. “This is a retaliation attack, which not only demonstrates that no organisation is safe from the group, but also the fact that they will go to any length to protect their image. “The group clearly wants to control the narrative around their activities, ensuring nothing is said that could dent their reputation. Just like any organisation, reputation is everything, even in the dark and unruly world of cybercrime.” Wright also said Oracle needs to act fast to mitigate the zero-day vulnerability that allegedly allowed this breach to occur. “It is essential Oracle takes steps to understand the [exploited] CVE [Common Vulnerabilities and Exposures] and whether it is already known to them. If not, an emergency update is essential,” he said. “Any gaps between this announcement and a patch being released will only leave organisations vulnerable. Other threats actors will use the gap to exploit the CVE and launch further attacks.” ShinyHunters has been linked to a number of high-profile breaches in recent months. In March, the prolific group claimed responsibility for a breach of the European Commission’s Europa.eu platform, in which a reported 350GB of data, across multiple databases, was accessed and stolen. In May, ShinyHunters claimed responsibility for hacking education management platform Canvas and issued a ransom to Canvas parent company Instructure. Some days later, Instructure reached an agreement with the group. Recently, ShinyHunters targeted rival cybercrime group Clop by hacking and defacing its ransomware leak site in an escalation of a dispute between the two groups. Xbox cutting 268 jobs amid widespread unit consolidation

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
As part of the consolidation, several Xbox studios will be consolidated under Call of Duty maker Activision. Microsoft’s gaming division Xbox has announced plans to layoff 268 employees, as the organisation enters a period of consolidation. The company intends to bring several of its studios under the banner of Activision, the publisher behind games such as Call of Duty, Crash Bandicoot and Spyro the Dragon. Studios Rare and Age of Empires-maker World’s Edge will be overseen by Activision. Activision was purchased by Microsoft in 2023 for $69bn and will also lead the development of the next Halo game, according to Matt Booty, Xbox’s chief content officer. The layoffs are expected to impact employees at Halo Studios, which will continue to support the Halo community and games in-market. Bethesda, which is responsible for popular game series Fallout and Elder Scrolls, will expand to include studio Obsidian Entertainment, which will continue to work on its current projects – which includes survival game Grounded and a new Fallout title. Video game developer King, which makes Candy Crush, will now absorb Microsoft Casual Games, while Playground and Turn 10 will merge as one studio to focus on the Forza and Fable franchises. In a statement regarding the layoffs, Booty said: “We are eliminating 268 roles across Halo Studios, other first-party studios and the XGS management and central functions layer. The actions completed since July, inclusive of the studio divestitures, bring us roughly three-quarters of the way through previously announced restructuring. “I am deeply grateful for what our colleagues have built, and I know how difficult today will be for those leaving and the teams around them. Second, we are reorganising our studios. The goal is to strengthen our franchises and games by operating fewer business units, aligning groups that already work closely together and focusing our publishing expertise.” Booty added: “Our focus should be on supporting those affected and treating one another with care and respect. I will spend time with our studios over the next week. At our 6 October town hall, we will come together as an All-Xbox team to reflect on our progress and look ahead to one of the strongest game lineups in our history.” In July, Microsoft announced that it was laying off 4,800 people, including 3,200 at Xbox, amid plans to cut costs and flatten its organisational structure in response to AI and a changing environment. Software Update Turns Samsung’s “AI Fridges” Into Useless Bricks
2026-09-23

Software Update Turns Samsung’s “AI Fridges” Into Useless Bricks

Futurism | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
It’s become common knowledge among home owners to avoid Samsung home appliances like the plague. They’re notorious for frequently breaking down, and fixing them can turn into an expensive ordeal — if it’s even an option at all. And after an entirely unnecessary and infuriating injection of AI, many unfortunate owners of a Samsung “Bespoke AI 4-Door” fridge were in for a rude surprise. Customers across South Korea found that their almost $4,000 appliance suddenly shut down after a faulty firmware update, turning them into an expensive — and fresh food ruining — brick. As Star News Korea reports, an internal panel screen displayed an error message for hours. Even unplugging the fridge and plugging it back in didn’t solve the issue as the cool air continued to escape. Worst of all, the malfunction occurred just before the Chuseok holiday break, a mid-autumn harvest festival that celebrates the full Moon and lasts for three days. Some owners said they had to throw out special delicacies set aside for the festivities. Adding insult to injury, angry customers were told the earliest servicing date was October 1, long after the holiday. The infuriating incident perfectly highlights how even the most mundane aspects of our lives are being turned into a buggy over-the-air software update-infested nightmare against our will. Samsung claims its allegedly “Smart” fridges know what’s inside of them, can recommend recipes, and even play music thanks to “AI” — features that we’d bet that almost no customers have ever touched. After all, electric refrigerators have been around for over a century, a significant and largely uncomplicated luxury of modern living that hasn’t changed much over the decades. It’s not just fridges being ruined by trendy tech. We’ve come across massively overengineered AI toothbrushes, AI toasters, and creepy facial recognition-infused AI doorbells as well. Fortunately, consumers still have plenty of choices to make when it comes to appliances. But as more companies bet on AI and insist on making our lives miserable by integrating it into every aspect of our lives, more reliable alternatives may soon become harder to come by. 2026-09-23

It Turns Out That Meta’s Muse AI Has an Amazingly Dirty Secret

Futurism | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
Meta brags that its new AI agent, Muse, can be an all-around “personal assistant” that takes care of everything in your mundane little life, ranging from online shopping, to managing emails, to keeping tabs on your finances — and, hell, even picking your wardrobe, disheveled slob that you are. Last week, it also announced something that actually sounded useful in an age where picking up the phone usually results in conversing with a bot: calling businesses on your behalf. No longer would you need to make an appointment, reserve a table, or wait through a phone tree on your own. Muse, Meta says, has you covered. It would be an impressive example of a practical AI use case for the average person — if it were really, truly, AI. Behind the scenes, Meta is having some of these calls being handled by humans in call centers, 404 Media reports, despite employees practically begging the company not to go through with the charade. It’s yet another example of classic AI industry subterfuge. Their tools are framed as powerful and self-sufficient, but in many cases it’s a façade being propped up by humans — often underpaid contractors, sometimes overseas — behind the scenes. For now, it’s unclear how many calls are being routed to humans, and the feature remains in its “dogfooding,” or testing, stage. The company abruptly announced the feature to employees and said it had “added a human agent layer for calls to get completed.” “Muse doesn’t just dial a number. It calls a business on your behalf, handles the conversation, completes your request, and reports back with a transcript and a summary,” the internal announcement, which was viewed by 404, reads. “It also no longer works alone. Muse is now able to hand requests to a trained human agent, who places the call and works it through.” “This is still a confidential, pre-launch product,” the notice adds. “Please don’t share it, or anything it producers, with anyone outside the company.” Employees were immediately skeptical, for good reason. By and large, they worried that word that Meta was even entertaining the idea could doom Muse’s image, while others raised the security concerns of sharing customer’s private requests internally. “Not sure what I am missing here but why is this a ‘feature?,'” one employee wrote on an internal message board, per 404, whose skepticism would prove prescient. “This has potential for so much negative PR. It could portray us as ‘their AI is not good enough so they still need humans’ kind of coverage for this launch.” Some practically threw themselves at their bosses feet, supplicating for a reversal. “This is absolutely going to create a ton of outcry if we publicly launch this as default-on,” another urged. “It will kill all the goodwill and organic press we’re getting from early adopters.” “Please think about how the headlines will scream ‘Meta uses humans behind the scenes to make calls’ or ‘Muse AI is actually independent contractors’ and result in a ton of bad press about the privacy and security of our data handling,” they continued, their prostration ever more despairing. “please PLEASE do not release this as-is and at least make this a user preference if we absolutely must launch this. Please.” In a statement to 404, Meta responded by framing the feature as a routine part of internal testing, though it didn’t rule out taking it mainstream. “While the response from employees has been overwhelmingly positive,” a spokesperson averred, “the entire point is to get feedback so we can implement safety and privacy protections and improve features before we release them publicly.” “We’re working with merchants to continue improving this potential calling feature,” the spokesperson added, “and will only roll it out when it’s ready and with the proper disclosures.” More on Meta: Meta Releases Uber-Creepy AI Chatbot as Its Platforms Crumble Under Grotesque Child Abuse

Former Google Exec Warns That AI Could Be Causing Terrible Harm to Minors, Even Worse Than Social Media
2026-09-23

Former Google Exec Warns That AI Could Be Causing Terrible Harm to Minors, Even Worse Than Social Media

Futurism | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
Never mind killer robots and sci-fi doomsday scenarios. A former Google executive says we need a slowdown in AI development now because the tech is already harming children at a scale that’s surpassing the damage wreaked by social media. “We find ourselves in a really terrible ​situation in terms of what AI is doing to kids,” Tom Siegel, the former vice president of trust and safety at Google, told Reuters. “The idea that the industry ​will figure it out by itself without outside help is unrealistic.” Major AI companies, Siegel says, are releasing their features without creating robust enough guardrails. On Tuesday, he announced he had joined the children’s online advocacy group Common Sense Media, serving as first executive director of its newly launched Youth AI Safety Institute. Some of the nonprofit’s backers, it’s worth noting, include Anthropic and the OpenAI Foundation. AI companies have come under fire for their role in so-called AI psychosis, cases in which an AI incites and drives a user’s delusional behavior. Numerous lawsuits allege that an older version of ChatGPT powered by GPT-4o even drove users to suicide. Many of these cases involve teenagers and young adults. Last year, 16-year-old Adam Raine killed himself after extensively discussing specific suicide methods with ChatGPT for months on end, his family alleged in lawsuit against OpenAI. Chat logs showed that Raine often confided his suicidal thoughts with the chatbot, and the chatbot in turn acted as a confidante, consoling him when he said he felt upset that his parents didn’t notice a rope mark from a hanging attempt. He died from a hanging technique that the AI discussed in detailed conversations with Raine. Meanwhile, many teens are treating AI companions as friends and even romantic companions. And educators are disheartened by the cognitive offloading caused by the chatbots, as students are increasingly turning to AI chatbots to answer questions and do their homework instead of thinking through assignments. Research has linked relying on AI tools to memory loss and diminished critical thinking skills. One study found that AI leads to lower brain activity during cognitive tasks like writing an essay. “I think we’re looking at very, very severe scenarios of harm being done,” Siegel told Reuters. A Google spokesperson responded by boasting that Google’s AI features are actually great for minors’ cognitive development. “Our AI Search features are an incredibly useful way for kids and teens to learn, explore and make sense of information and the world,” the spokesperson told Reuters. An OpenAI spokesperson affected more concern, explaining that the company uses “account and behavioral signals” and self-reported ages to detect underage user. “While no single age-assurance method is perfect, we continuously test and improve these systems, including ⁠against attempts to ​bypass safeguards,” they added. Stanford University Caught Using AI to Create Fake Black Student on Banners Hung Around Campus
2026-09-23

Stanford University Caught Using AI to Create Fake Black Student on Banners Hung Around Campus

Futurism | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
Stanford University was caught using AI to swap out a student in a photo and replace him with a Black woman who didn’t exist. That edited photo, which shows the students — both the fake one and a pair of real ones — smiling and holding up plates of food, was then plastered around campus to promote the university’s dining hall. At first, Billy Ramirez, the student who was edited out of the photo, thought it was “hilarious.” But Ramirez, who is Hispanic, soon felt “silenced and erased from a representation that was supposed to include me,” he told Stanford Review, the student-run newspaper which first reported the astonishing screwup. Race swapping wasn’t the only AI alteration in poor taste. The two other students in the photo, a young Asian woman and white man, were essentially yassified to appear thinner and more conventionally attractive. According to reporting by the San Francisco Chronicle, the AI-touched-up photo was used in “Welcome Home” banners around the university, but Ramirez said that the posters, which were once “all over campus,” are now being taken down. “I love this school, but I don’t love when they do things like this,” he told the newspaper. Swapping in a fake Black student didn’t “reflect reality,” he added. “It’s not as diverse as they make it out to be.” Stanford’s Residential & Dining Enterprises department, SFChronicle found, has previously used AI to create dining posters promoting different deals. (The over-greebled chow in the posters is an unmistakable telltale of the horrifying AI food art that’s been receiving loads of backlash lately.) A Stanford spokesperson confirmed that AI was used to edit the images and that it violated Stanford’s AI policies. “The use of AI in producing or altering images of Stanford people, events, research, facilities, or achievements is strictly prohibited,” the spokesperson told SFChronicle. “Both the alteration and lack of disclosure in this case violate that policy. We are working to provide additional training for all staff and ensure proper review of all materials.” It’s yet another example of AI being used to casually rewrite reality, perhaps in the cynical hope of achieving a “perfect” representation of something. The result, beyond being a lie, is often cheap, garish, and conspicuous. Stanford Review‘s view was that the dining hall “likely believed [the changes] were subtle enough for no one to notice.” Ramirez, in all, sounded jaded and a little bemused. “It is Silicon Valley,” he told SFChronicle. “I’m not as surprised given the culture of Stanford revolves heavily around AI usage,” adding that some of his professors regularly use Anthropic’s Claude chatbot. AI Community Linked to Disturbing Culture of Sexual Assault
2026-09-23

AI Community Linked to Disturbing Culture of Sexual Assault

Futurism | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
The tech world’s potential future founders are living out hedonistic existences in giant Silicon Valley mansions, where they live with a dozen and even more like-minded individuals, and where life is more a less a constant party. These so-called hacker houses, though, have a dark side. A recent New York Times investigation has uncovered several harassment cases and rape allegations stemming from hacker houses. The most prominent of these is the AGI House — short for “artificial general intelligence” — in Hillsborough, where police have logged 37 incidents since 2022. At least 17 were related to the often large and extravagant parties and events that were regularly being thrown on the property. In December, a woman called the Hillsborough police station at 2 in the morning from a Lyft, reporting that she had been trapped at the AGI House by a male resident who tried to force her to have sex. The man told her she was old and “would be alone forever,” according to the police reported viewed by the NYT.  He was “persistent in attempting to convince” the woman “to have sex with him, and she repeatedly declined.” She escaped by climbing over a gate, unharmed. The AGI House was founded by Jeremy Nixon, a former Google researcher who’s established other tech homes, with the vision of having “20 AI hackers, living in the same epic house for three months,” he told the NYT. The domicile was originally called NeoGenesis, but rebranded after OpenAI released ChatGPT and kickstarted the AI boom. And before NeoGenesis, he co-founded a hacker house called Genesis in 2019. Genesis had a dark history of its own. Not wanting for debauchery, it was also a hub for parties, with one 19-year-old tech entrepreneur claiming he used to sneak into parties when he was just 13. “It was pretty crazy,” he told the NYT. Yehong Zhu, 30, filed a police reporting accusing a former Genesis house member of rape and assault in December 2021. Zhu has lived in four tech houses, and her view of them as utopic spaces for entrepreneurs was shattered by her experience. The case is still under investigation, and the accused Genesis resident hasn’t left the house and can’t be located, she said. “I’m far from the only one this has happened to,” Zhu, who founded her own startup called Zette AI, told the NYT. To lay out the stakes, these hacker houses aren’t random gatherings. Some are directly run by tech executives like OpenAI CEO Sam Altman, who has advised one called the Residency. AGI House regularly had tech industry royalty show up for dinners, including Google co-founder Sergey Brin. In a direct way, they’re responsible for fostering the culture and beliefs that the current and upcoming generations of AI industry workers, if not leaders, are being instilled with, along with building the network of connections that’ll shape their careers. The next Sam Altman or Elon Musk is probably spending their formative young adult years in one of these debaucherous dens. The people running them are well aware of their outsized influence. The AGI House’s current manager, venture capitalist Rocky Yu, is in an ongoing legal battle with a former manager over who owns the rights to the AGI House brand, the NYT noted. There’re other signs that all is not well behind closed doors. Police were also asked to perform welfare checks on at least four different occasions at the AGI House. On one occasion, they even sent an ambulance to the house. More on AI: Is There a Secret Reason for the Industry-Wide AI Slowdown?

Crowd of Angry Citizens Loses It After City Council Fast-Tracks Resolution on Flock AI Cameras
2026-09-23

Crowd of Angry Citizens Loses It After City Council Fast-Tracks Resolution on Flock AI Cameras

Futurism | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
A crowd of anxious community members in Springfield, Missouri turned downright hostile on Monday, leaping to their feet after city council members rubber-stamped a resolution around Flock surveillance cameras. Footage published by local channel KY3 captured the moment counselors voted 5-3 to send the resolution on Automatic License Plate Readers (ALPRs) to the city’s planning committee, functionally bypassing any chance at a public hearing. The audience immediately turned hostile following the vote, with throngs of attendees screaming out at the top of their lungs as they filed out of the meeting hall in protest. “Worthless!” one unidentified community member bellowed before storming off. “What I’m trying to do is get people answers to some real question that they’ve brought to us for months,” said councilman Brandon Jenson, one of the councilmembers who tried to open the floor for public comment. “It’s a shame that it comes down to this sort of, I guess, theater… to try and help get those answers — this isn’t how government is supposed to run.” “It’s just disappointing,” Jenson continued. “And we’re not even allowing the folks to speak who clearly came out to share their voices tonight.” The ultimate fate of the resolution is still up in the air. If passed, it would mandate a review of the city police department’s use of ALPRs, which have become rife with abuse by other law enforcement agencies throughout the United States. “It just makes me wonder where we’re trying to go if something as straightforward as this gets referred to a policy committee to be shelved until who knows when,” Isaac Protiva, an organizer with the anti-ALPR group Flock Out SGF told KY3 after the hearing. “It just makes me question where this city goes and honestly makes me question how much I want to invest in this city as a young person that moved here from deeper in the Ozarks,” Protiva continued. If Amazon doesn’t know how to eliminate carbon emissions, then who does?
2026-09-23

If Amazon doesn’t know how to eliminate carbon emissions, then who does?

TechCrunch | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Disrupt ticket savings of up to $200 + 50% off a second ends Sept 25, 11:59 p.m. PT. REGISTER HERE. Amazon’s chief sustainability officer said Tuesday the company doesn’t know how it will achieve its goal of net-zero carbon emissions by 2040. “We are still striving towards that target,” Kara Hurst said at an Axios event, adding later that she is “not going to sit here and say, ‘We know all the ways that we’re going to do this.’” The admission was both frank and realistic. It’s also a bit disappointing. If Amazon doesn’t have a plan to decarbonize, then who does? Amazon is one of the largest companies in the world, with last year’s revenue up 12% to $717 billion — about the size of Ireland’s economy. Its rise is illustrative of how, as UN Secretary General António Guterres has pointed out, power has shifted from governments to a few corporations. As power moves, responsibility follows. The question is, how much responsibility does Amazon bear? “This isn’t a question of companies versus governments,” Hurst told TechCrunch in a written reply to questions after the event. “Tackling climate change at the scale it demands takes both, and it takes them working together.” She added, “Where we can lead, we do.” It’s true that Amazon has not been sitting still. In her talk yesterday, Hurst pointed out that the company is “one of the largest purchasers of clean energy in the world for the last several years running” with 42 gigawatts in its portfolio. She also said that Amazon has reduced its carbon intensity, or how much pollution it generates per dollar of revenue, though that claim depends on the time frame. Last year, carbon intensity rose compared with 2024, even though it stayed below where it was in 2022. Amazon is a sprawling business, and as a result, its carbon emissions are spread across nearly every sector of the economy, from aviation and AI to agriculture and construction. The company needs suppliers in every sector to achieve its goal. “This is not something we can do alone,” Hurst said at the Axios event. The company acknowledges that its commitments are its own. “We hold ourselves accountable to The Climate Pledge goal of net-zero carbon by 2040. That commitment hasn’t changed,” Hurst told TechCrunch. But Amazon has also made choices that undermine its leadership on the issue. Earlier this year, an investigation revealed the company’s plans to build a 7.65 gigawatt natural gas power plant to power an AI data center campus. The project’s 35 turbines could release up to 33 million tons of carbon dioxide annually, making it the largest single source of that pollution in the U.S. With few exceptions, companies prioritize revenue growth over climate commitments. For a company like Amazon, which operates the largest cloud service in the world, AI is apparently a revenue driver that’s impossible to pass up. The climate tech world has been hoping that, as clean power gets cheaper, the market would work its magic to lower carbon emissions. There have been a few success stories, but as the AI rush has shown, that approach has its limits. Even Amazon, one of the leaders in the space, bet big on natural gas. In the process, it essentially said that climate change is something for future generations of executives to deal with. Sound familiar? Bessemer Venture Partners announced on Wednesday that it has raised $5.75 billion across two new funds to accelerate its investment across all parts of the AI stack. The firm, which has backed companies such as Anthropic, Cognition, Legora, Perplexity, Ramp, Shopify, and Waymo, dedicated $1.75 billion for seed and early-stage investing and the remaining $4 billion for growth startups. Bessemer has been well-known as a leading VC in the SaaS era, with a particular knack for picking enterprise winners, like Box, Docusign, and Gainsight. Since 2022, it has invested in more than 260 AI-native companies. So far, the firm has invested $3 billion into AI-related startups, it says, including those focused on compute, infrastructure, foundation models, dev tools, app-layer startups, and agentic tech. Now, Bessemer positions all things AI as the opportunity of a lifetime — a common sentiment, until the next big trendy tech revolution comes around, of course. “AI-native companies are scaling faster than any category of technology we’ve backed before,” Bessemer Partner Byron Deeter said in the company’s funding announcement. Modal Motors is trying to cut China out of electric motors entirely
2026-09-23

Modal Motors is trying to cut China out of electric motors entirely

TechCrunch | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Disrupt ticket savings of up to $200 + 50% off a second ends Sept 25, 11:59 p.m. PT. REGISTER HERE. Lately, it seems like everyone is talking about bringing manufacturing back to the United States, especially when it comes to hard tech like drones and robotics. Michael Van Steenburg thinks he’s ahead of the game. Van Steenburg spent the last decade building all kinds of electric motors in the U.S. and North America, and now he wants to do it again with his new startup, Modal Motors. This time, he wants to ditch rare-earth magnets entirely, in a bid to further separate the U.S. from China’s supply chain. As one of the founders in Startup Battlefield 200 who will compete at TechCrunch Disrupt next month, he thinks it’s an urgent pitch to make. “China’s already flexed its muscles a couple times,” Van Steenburg said, referring to the country restricting access to rare-earth products. There are other companies working on setting up electric motor manufacturing and supply chains in the U.S., but Van Steenburg argues they’re doing it the wrong way by continuing to rely on rare-earth magnets. Domestic supply of those materials is still years away, he said. “Other people are ‘reshoring,’ [but] they’re basically knocking off Chinese motors and just doing it in the U.S. But they’re hoping that the U.S. rare-earth magnet system is going to get up to speed fast enough,” he said. “It’s going to still be a while longer before they can actually replace China.” Modal Motors has two products that it’s developing ahead of Disrupt. One is a small electric motor meant for drones that can also work in fans, blowers, and even actuators. It’s designed to “minimize mass while delivering maximum torque density and efficiency,” according to the startup’s website. The more radical design is what Modal refers to as the “Hybrid Enigma Drive,” a planar — or flat — electric motor that can fit in lower-profile spaces. Van Steenburg is drawing on decades of experience in the automotive industry to develop these motors and get them customer-ready. But there’s also an irony in the work Van Steenburg is doing to make Modal Motors’ products a China-free option; in the late 1990s, he worked on a hybrid gas-electric drivetrain that China wanted to put in its so-called “People’s Car.” “It’s kind of comical,” he said, thinking back on that experience and how much the world has changed. What hasn’t changed, he said, is his outsider streak and strong conviction about doing things that are hard. “I always tell people that the clock started ticking 25 years ago, and in that whole time, I’ve watched a slow-motion train wreck as China’s been going around the world, grabbing mines, grabbing mineral rights, doing all these things, and slowly but surely monopolizing the entire rare-earth supply chain,” he said. 2026-09-23

Disney+ and Hulu add to the growing trend of streaming inflation

TechCrunch | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Disrupt ticket savings of up to $200 + 50% off a second ends Sept 25, 11:59 p.m. PT. REGISTER HERE. Streaming is getting more expensive again. Disney is the latest media giant to raise prices for its streaming services, adding to a growing pattern across the industry. The increases come roughly a year after Disney’s last major streaming price hike. Disney is raising the price of its ad-free Disney+ and Hulu bundle to $21.99 a month, up from $19.99, whereas the ad-free stand-alone plans are each rising to $21.49 per month, up from $18.99. The company is also increasing the price of its stand-alone ad-supported Disney+ and Hulu plans to $12.49 a month. The new prices are reflected on Disney+’s support page. Bloomberg was the first to report the changes. Disney+ and Hulu are hardly alone. Streaming companies have spent the past several years steadily increasing prices. Peacock and Apple TV also raised prices in August, while Netflix increased prices earlier this year. When Disney+ launched in 2019, its basic monthly subscription cost $6.99. Since then, the service has gradually moved further away from that intro price as Disney has tried to turn streaming into a profitable part of its entertainment business. The latest increases come as streaming remains an increasingly important part of Disney’s overall business. In its third-quarter 2026 results, Disney said entertainment streaming revenue from Disney+ and Hulu rose 11% to $5.5 billion, driven by subscriber growth and the impact of previous price increases. At the same time, Disney appears to be exploring new ways to expand its streaming business beyond simply raising subscription prices. The company has reportedly explored the possibility of introducing a free tier for Disney+. This could put the company in more direct competition with platforms such as YouTube and Tubi, which have been capturing a growing share of consumers’ viewing time. Earlier this month, Disney+ also introduced “Playlists,” a new feature designed to help users discover and continuously watch curated selections of content. The company is making changes on the technology side as well. Disney recently hired Karandeep Anand as its first-ever chief technology officer. Anand is the former CEO of Character.AI, a company Disney previously accused of infringing on its intellectual property. Everything You Need to Know About SpaceX's Wildlife Refuge Land Swap with the Trump Administration
2026-09-23

Everything You Need to Know About SpaceX's Wildlife Refuge Land Swap with the Trump Administration

TIME | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings SpaceX hopes to expand in Texas wilderness area. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings SpaceX has not always distinguished itself as a good environmental citizen. Part of that is unavoidable. You can’t launch 170 rockets per year—as the company did in 2025—without releasing a lot of black soot into the upper atmosphere and creating a lot of light and noise around the launch site, which can be harmful to local wildlife. Environmental organizations have mounted multiple legal challenges against the company over the years to enjoin or minimize its freedom to launch until multiple corrective measures are taken. Now SpaceX faces another day in court. On Sept. 21 a judge ruled that a proposed land swap with the federal government that would allow the company to expand its launch pads, factory, and related infrastructure into a protected wildlife area in Texas could go ahead. That might have settled the issue but the environmental groups that sued to stop this, plan to appeal—and they believe they have a strong case. On June 10, a group of plaintiffs filed suit against the Department of the Interior to block a Trump Administration plan to cede SpaceX 725 acres of land in the Lower Rio Grande National Wildlife Refuge hard up against the company’s Starbase factory and launch site in Boca Chica, Texas, in exchange for 692 acres the company owns in the Laguna Atascosa National Wildlife Refuge about 20 miles north of Starbase. The land that would be annexed by SpaceX is not only home to thousands of species of plants and animals including the ocelot, which is endangered in the U.S.; it is also considered sacred by the Native American Carrizo/Comecrudo Nation of Texas, which is a plaintiff in the suit. What’s more, the tract has cultural and historical significance as it includes the Palmito Ranch Battlefield, the site of the last land battle of the Civil War. The fact that the acreage that SpaceX gets will not be a gift, but rather an exchange for land the company currently owns—taking that latter plot off the table for development—does not solve the problem, says Laiken Jordahl, public lands advocate for the Center for Biological Diversity, the lead plaintiff in the lawsuit. “The whole point of the Lower Rio Grande Valley National Wildlife Refuge is to establish a chain of connected habitats along the river that allows for the migration of ocelots and other wildlife,” he argues. “This will cut out one of the key east-west areas of wildlife connectivity, and in return, we're getting a smaller amount of land in a different area.” On. Sept. 21, Judge Fernando Rodriuguez, Jr., of the U.S. District Court for the Southern District of Texas, denied the Center for Biological Diversity’s request for a preliminary injunction that would prevent the move, ruling that the plaintiffs did not have standing to sue. In their filing, the plaintiffs argued that the land swap would cause them irreparable harm since they would lose access to those acres and the freedom to observe wildlife there. The Interior Department countered that the land was already closed to the public as part of its wildlife refuge status, so the plaintiffs suffered no loss. The plaintiffs, in their filing, anticipated this finding and sought to rebut it in advance with 2014 case law. “[T]he affected individual,” they wrote, “need not always have direct access to the area at issue. Individuals can possess a cognizable interest ‘in observing the landscape from surrounding areas[.]’” Rodriguez—appointed to the federal bench by President Donald Trump during his first term—was unmoved, and if anything, saw SpaceX as the potentially aggrieved party. The requested injunction, he wrote, “would impose uncertainty into and would result in modifications to SpaceX’s development plans, placing additional hardship on the company’s ability to meet milestones and contractual obligations.” “We are obviously, extraordinarily disappointed in the judge's decision,” says Jordahl. “This effectively clears the way for the bulldozers to start destroying the refuge land. But the judge did not rule on our overall claims; it was just on the injunction. So yeah, the challenge will press forward.” (The denial of a preliminary injunction falls short of the judge actually ruling on whether the proposed land swap violates federal law—which the plaintiffs claim it does.) It’s not for nothing that environmental groups worry about the impact of SpaceX’s south Texas site. That's where the company launches its massive Starship rocket—a 33-engine behemoth that stands 40-stories tall and puts out up to 18 million lbs of thrust, more than twice as much as NASA’s Space Launch System, the second most powerful rocket in the world. Four minutes into Starship’s inaugural launch on April 20, 2023, it exploded spectacularly, raining debris downward—most of which fell into the Gulf of Mexico. But the mere ignition of the nearly three dozen engines on the concrete launch apron did plenty of harm of its own. “Impacts from the launch include numerous large concrete chunks, stainless steel sheets, metal and other objects hurled thousands of feet away along with a plume cloud of pulverized concrete that deposited material up to 6.5 miles northwest of the pad site,” the U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service wrote in an email to the media at the time. “Approximately 385 acres of debris was found on both SpaceX’s facility and in the surrounding Boca Chica State Park, and a 3.5-acre fire burned on park land south of the launch site.” Jordahl is not worried just about similar environmental damage, but the harm to be done when those bulldozers he mentions begin to roll. “I've heard different things [about what SpaceX wants to do with the land], from additional manufacturing areas to warehouses to worker housing,” says Jordahl. “We haven't gotten any clear and obvious plans. But the way that they've been treating the land out there already is telling. They pave over everything. They turn it into an industrial manufacturing zone.” SpaceX did not respond to a request for comment. The harm won’t stop there. As SpaceX expands into the refuge, more land that is currently a comfortable distance from the complex will be exposed to the damage the launches can do. SpaceX claims it has cured the problems that made the first launch such a mess, installing a water-cooled metal deflection plate and a retaining wall to control debris. The other 12 Starship launches that have taken place since the inaugural one have indeed caused far less damage to the surroundings. If that mitigates the environmental damage the rocket can do, however, the planned launch cadence exacerbates it. In August, SpaceX founder and CEO Elon Musk told Aviation Week that he envisions daily Starship launches—carrying crew, satellites, and other cargo to space—as early as 2027. More headsnapping was his 2025 boast on X, that “In about 6 or 7 years, there will be days where Starship launches more than 24 times in 24 hours.” Clearly, not all of those launches could come from the Texas site, but SpaceX has other launch facilities at Vandenberg Space Force Base in California, and dedicated pads at the Kennedy Space Center and the Cape Canaveral Space Force Station in Florida. A company so large, with more than a $2 trillion valuation, could always build more launchpads at more coastal sites whenever it chooses. The Texas base is making news at the moment, not simply for what it means for this potential development site, but for others elsewhere in the U.S. The Trump Administration is currently pursuing a similar land swap with a private land developer, involving a strip of land in Yosemite National Park for an undeveloped parcel in California so that residents in a planned private housing complex near the park will have easier access to it. Yet another land exchange is under consideration that would open up a federally designated wilderness area on Georgia’s Cumberland island to the construction of luxury homes. “This [Texas] land transfer is part of a broader scheme by this administration to give away public lands to private interests,” says Jordahl. “The precedent that this transfer will set is extremely concerning when it comes to corporations setting their sights on and taking over public lands across America.
TIME
How the U.K. Is Supporting Saudi Arabia In Its Fight Against the Houthis
2026-09-23

How the U.K. Is Supporting Saudi Arabia In Its Fight Against the Houthis

TIME | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Crew in the cockpit of an RAF Voyager air-to-air refuelling (AAR) aircraft above RAF Akrotiri in Cyprus on March 27, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Saudi Arabia will have assistance from a key Western ally in its fight against Yemen’s Iran-backed Houthis, as Britain is providing military support to the Kingdom in a bid to help restore stability in the region and ease pressures on the global energy supply. “We’re working with our friends to try and deliver that de-escalation,” British Defense Minister Luke Pollard said in an interview Tuesday. The “purely defensive” deployment of an RAF Voyager to Saudi Arabia is intended to “ensure there’s no further escalation in the Red Sea, which could further risk our fuel prices,” he added. The international assistance comes following weeks of fighting between the Saudi-backed government of Yemen and the Houthis, which shattered a four-year informal cease-fire in the country's civil war. The Houthi rebels have made significant advances along the Red Sea coastline as they seek to tighten their grip on the Bab el-Mandeb, a critical maritime chokepoint that links Asia and Europe via the Red Sea and Suez Canal. With global energy supplies already under strain due to the disruption in the Strait of Hormuz amid the Iran war, the strangling of another trade route could further impact oil and fuel prices. “Much of our trade, both in goods and in energy, comes through that part of the world. And so, what happens in that part of the Middle East is absolutely vital for our own national security and our own economic security,” Pollard said, explaining why Britain responded to the Kingdom’s request for help. When talking to reporters en route to the United Nations General Assembly in New York, ahead of his sit-down with President Donald Trump on Tuesday, U.K. Prime Minister Andy Burnham also cited the cost of living “pressures” at home as a key reason for the support and said he was “acting to secure the interests of Britain and the wider region.” Burnham emphasized that it’s a “time-limited agreement in terms of the assistance that we will provide, but we will keep it under review.” The support extended to Saudi Arabia marks Burnham’s first military deployment since he became Prime Minister in July. However, it’s far from the first time the U.K. has offered assistance to Saudi Arabia, as the two nations share a close military alliance. The Ministry of Defense on Tuesday confirmed it had agreed to “provide an RAF Voyager air-to-air refuelling aircraft to support Saudi defensive activity” in the region. According to the British Royal Air Force (RAF), a Voyager provides strategic air support by allowing jets and other aircraft to refuel while in flight. Andreas Krieg, a senior lecturer in defense studies at King's College London, says the Voyager “basically allows the Saudi Air Force to stay in the air over Yemen for longer without having to fly a single sortie and then returning to base.” “It increases the operational tempo in terms of targeting strikes and being more flexible, and more agile in responding to changing situations on the ground,” he tells TIME. According to Pollard, the Voyager is set to operate from the British RAF base in Cyprus, which was targeted by Iran earlier this year, and will support the British-built Typhoon jets used by Saudi Arabia. “That will help them with their defensive actions against the Houthis, who have been attacking not just the capital of Saudi Arabia in Riyadh, but also the Republic of Yemen as well,” he said. Britain has historically held the role of “guarantor for maritime freedom of navigation all around the world,” says Krieg, describing the U.K.’s latest attempt to ease tensions over the Red Sea as an example of the government “stepping up to the plate.” “We're not just helping a partner in the region,” he adds. “We're also helping ourselves, because the Houthis having the Bab al-Mandeb is not something that the U.K. can tolerate.” Krieg says the move signifies a small success for Saudi Arabia and other Gulf states as they seek to strengthen bilateral security relationships beyond their traditional reliance on the U.S. This is “the worst security situation the Saudis have been in [for] a very long time,” he notes. “Gulf states are trying to supplement the existing U.S. capabilities with all kinds of [other] agreements, like the Mecca pact,” he says, referencing a trilateral defense security pact made between Pakistan, Saudi Arabia, and Turkey in August. “The U.K. wants to be seen as being active and being there when a friend is in need.” The U.K. and Saudi Arabia share an alliance that dates back to almost a century ago. “It's a very long, strategic partnership,” says Krieg. “It goes back to the early days of the current Kingdom, where Britain, at the time, was still the only superpower in the world, and was controlling the waterways and a lot of the territories in Arabia, including in South Yemen.” Britain was among the first governments to recognize the independent territories of Saudi Arabia through the Treaty of Jeddah in 1927, which helped solidify the boundaries of the nascent kingdom ahead of the formation of modern Saudi Arabia in 1932. Decades later, the U.K. supported the Saudi-led military coalition in its campaign against the Houthis from 2015 until the 2022 truce. According to the Campaign Against Arms Trade (CAAT), the published value of U.K. arms export licenses to Saudi Arabia from the start of the bombing campaign in March 2015 through the April 2022 truce was £7.1 billion ($9.4 billion). “The two main Western backers of the Saudi Kingdom are the Americans and the British,” says David Wearing, an assistant professor in international relations at the University of Sussex. “From the British and the American point of view, the Middle East is strategically important because it's a huge source of global energy suppliers, oil, and gas.” In late 2023, the Houthis started disrupted shipping in the Red Sea, targeting cargo ships they said were linked to Israel, in what they described as a show of support for Palestinians in Gaza. Amid concerns over the impact on the world economy, the U.S. and U.K. joined forces in January 2024, striking multiple Houthi targets in Yemen. In April 2025, the U.K. government participated in another joint operation with the U.S. against a Houthi military facility in Yemen. In March of this year, the U.K. deployed a Sky Sabre, the army’s medium range air defense missile system, to Saudi Arabia to defend the Kingdom against Iranian missile and drone attacks. The U.K. government's Strategic Export Controls Annual Report of 2025 described the military relationship with Saudi Arabia as a “longstanding” partnership providing “Typhoon, Tornado, and Hawk aircraft, mine countermeasure vessels, and associated munitions, infrastructure, logistics and manpower support packages.” BAE Systems—a major British defense, aerospace, and security company—said in its own 2025 report that Saudi Arabia was its largest customer by sales in its air business. Saudi Arabia has reportedly sought assistance from allies to stave off Houthi advances. France, Pakistan, and Egypt issued strong statements condemning the Houthi attacks, but have not publicly announced new military support for the Kingdom. U.S. President Donald Trump is understood to have received requests from Saudi Crown Prince Mohammed bin Salman to strike the Houthi rebels. According to the New York Times, Trump reportedly considered launching strikes against the Houthis over the weekend, before ultimately deciding against it. TIME has reached out to the White House for comment. Asked Tuesday why Washington has held back from striking the Houthis, Secretary of State Marco Rubio said it’s “a very dangerous and complex” conflict. “If it comes down to it, the United States will always stand with Saudi Arabia and support them because we have an agreement with them to do so,” he said, referencing the U.S. economic and defense partnership with Saudi Arabia signed last November, that deepened co-operation between the two countries. Global energy supplies have been under increasing strain since the start of the Iran war on Feb. 28, as Tehran has leveraged its chokehold over the Strait of Hormuz, through which around a fifth of global oil production flowed prior to the conflict. In the past few weeks, the Houthis have captured Yemen’s port city of Mokha and the strategic Perim Island, which sits in the Bab el-Mandeb Strait. If the Houthis were to assume control of the strait, shipping companies could be forced to travel via South Africa’s Cape of Good Hope—a longer, costlier route. The volume of crude oil and petroleum liquids moving through the Bab el-Mandeb increased sharply earlier this year, as the route has been used as an alternative to the Strait of Hormuz. The shipments have risen from 5.6 million barrels per day in the first quarter of 2026 to 8.1 million per day in the second quarter, according to the U.S. Energy Information Administration (EIA). However, recent crossings have been marred by uncertainty over the potential threat posed by the Houthis. Data shared with TIME by Kpler, a commodities data and analytics firm, showed an estimated 34 vessels transited the Bab el-Mandeb on Sept. 15, a drop from the estimated 53 crossings recorded on July 15th. A spokesperson for the Houthis on Sept. 11 insisted the waterway will remain free to roam “with the exception of Saudi ships.” Yet concerns among global leaders and shipping companies remain. Burnham and other U.K. officials have framed Britain’s defensive support partly around the potential economic consequences at home. The average price of petrol rose by 9.1 pence per litre between July and August 2026, compared with a rise of 0.3 pence per litre between July and August 2025, according to data from the Office of National Statistics. Wearing says that because oil is traded on a global market, supply disruptions in one region can push up benchmark prices worldwide, with those costs cascading through the broader economy. “What Americans call gas and what we call petrol, if that goes up, think how many goods to market are taken by heavy lorries on petrol. It can push up the price of everything: shipping, road transit, energy, all kinds of industrial production and many consumer products,” he says. “Oil is the lifeblood of the world economy, the longer you restrict the supply, the higher the price goes, the more inflationary pressure there is on everything, and that affects Britain, but it affects the whole system. It's like a heart attack in a human body, suddenly it's not being pumped round."
