A Debt Ceiling Fight Looms in 2027. Could a Democratic Congress Make it the Last One?
2026-09-23

A Debt Ceiling Fight Looms in 2027. Could a Democratic Congress Make it the Last One?

TIME | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Rep. Brendan Boyle, the ranking Democrat on the House Budget Committee, listens as Republican Chairman Jodey Arrington speaks during a markup hearing in the Cannon House Office Building on July 16, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Sometime next year, the U.S. government will hit its debt ceiling again. It's a moment that often plays out as a high-stakes partisan battle, with lawmakers demanding concessions from the President under the threat of a U.S. default. If Democrats win control of one or both chambers of Congress, the next debt ceiling vote could yield another standoff. Or, just maybe, it could be an unexpected moment of bipartisanship. President Trump has repeatedly called for eliminating the debt ceiling, putting him at odds with most of his party, but in line with a significant number of Democrats in Congress. The top Democrat on the House Budget Committee sees an opportunity to finally ditch the mechanism for good. “I will not vote to simply kick the can down the road again and just raise the debt ceiling for another 18 months,” Rep. Brendan Boyle of Pennsylvania tells TIME in an interview. “My next vote on the debt ceiling will only be to permanently reform it to end the dysfunction around it once and for all.” After nearly a decade in the House, it would be the Philadelphia Democrat's first time voting against raising the debt ceiling. Congress last approved raising the debt ceiling in July 2025, by $5 trillion to $41.1 trillion. The Treasury Department is expected to hit that limit some time in 2027, although estimates vary. One projection puts the likely window between late winter and mid-summer. Once the limit is reached, the Treasury can rely on cash reserves and “extraordinary measures” for several more months before reaching the so-called X-date, when it could no longer meet all of the government’s obligations on time. While lawmakers often frame debt ceiling votes as tests of fiscal restraint, raising the debt ceiling doesn’t actually authorize new spending or determine how much the government borrows in the first place. The debt ceiling caps the Treasury Department’s ability to borrow the money the U.S. government needs to pay for obligations Congress has already approved. Past debt ceiling fights have led to credit downgrades and major drops in the stock market. Rep. Tim Burchett, a Tennessee Republican who voted for the last increase, subsequently called the debt ceiling “a joke” and “a relic of a bygone era.” Other Republicans, including outgoing Rep. Chip Roy of Texas, continue to argue that the ceiling should be preserved as a negotiating tool. During Biden’s presidency, Trump urged his party to preserve the debt ceiling so they could use it as leverage in negotiations. Since returning to the White House, however, he has repeatedly called for eliminating the ceiling. “I am very pleased to announce that, after all of these years, I agree with Senator Elizabeth Warren on SOMETHING," Trump posted on Truth Social in June. "The Debt Limit should be entirely scrapped to prevent an Economic catastrophe. It is too devastating to be put in the hands of political people that may want to use it despite the horrendous effect it could have on our Country and, indirectly, even the World." If Democrats win the House, Boyle is all but assured to be chairman of the House Budget Committee, which oversees the debt limit votes. He sees Trump’s willingness to act on the issue as a golden opportunity. His preferred approach would not abolish the ceiling outright, but largely transfer the responsibility of raising it to the Treasury secretary, who would be able to suspend the debt ceiling for up to two years. Congress would retain the ability to block the suspensions through a joint resolution that would have to be signed by the president. Boyle says his solution, which he introduced as the Debt Ceiling Reform Act in 2023, has drawn support from members from both parties. “I've had a number of House Republicans quietly tell me that they want to see the debt ceiling finally resolved in a manner that I propose because they're sick and tired of voting on it,” he says. The White House declined to comment on whether Trump would be open to working with Democrats to eliminate or reform the debt ceiling. For most of its history, the U.S. did not have a debt ceiling. First adopted in 1917, raising the ceiling has become a bigger political headache in recent decades. In 2011, House Republicans used the approaching borrowing limit to force President Barack Obama into an agreement that imposed spending caps and created a bipartisan process for pursuing additional deficit reduction. But the standoff also brought the country close to default and was followed by the first downgrade of U.S. government debt. Twelve years later, Republicans again used the debt ceiling to extract concessions from a Democratic president. The 2023 agreement negotiated by Speaker Kevin McCarthy and President Joe Biden capped discretionary spending for two years, rescinded more than $27 billion in unspent pandemic aid and $1.4 billion in IRS funding, and tightened work requirements for certain SNAP recipients. In return, Biden secured a suspension of the debt ceiling through the end of 2024, removing the immediate threat of default. The agreement was estimated to reduce deficits by roughly $1.5 trillion over a decade, but it also temporarily spiked short-term borrowing costs and led to another credit downgrade by a different credit agency. “It is inevitable that the next time there's a Democrat in the White House and there's a Republican-controlled Congress, they will do exactly what they did to Barack Obama and exactly what they did to Joe Biden,” Boyle says. But the situation may be reversed next year, with Democrats potentially controlling one or both chambers of Congress and a Republican in the White House. Some Democrats are already discussing the prospect of extracting concessions from Trump to secure a debt ceiling hike. During a recent retreat of the Congressional Progressive Caucus, Boyle was asked to give a presentation on the issue in order to encourage members to start thinking about the looming topic. While Boyle hasn’t spoken recently with Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries about the party’s strategy around the debt ceiling, he notes that Jeffries signed a 2022 letter with him and other House Democrats urging Congressional leaders to “permanently end the threat that the federal debt ceiling poses to our economy and our standing in the world.” Boyle has already been laying the groundwork for a broader role on the House Budget Committee. During the current Congress, his office built a district-by-district database tracking the effects of Trump Administration policies on household costs and health coverage. The data played a central role in Democrats’ messaging strategy around healthcare and the 2025 government shutdown, as Democratic offices used the data to show voters in their districts how the expiration of enhanced Affordable Care Act tax credits could affect them. As chairman, Boyle wants the committee to work to overhaul a congressional budget process that has turned dysfunctional. He also wants to reassert Congress' constitutional power over federal spending, as the Trump Administration has repeatedly worked to withhold or redirect dollars lawmakers had appropriated. He intends to call Russell Vought, the director of the Office of Management and Budget and a central player in that Administration strategy, to a hearing on the issue. “I think he's the most dangerous person in America that most Americans have never heard of,” Boyle says of Vought. The potential chairmanship is also central to Boyle’s own calculation about his future in Congress. As Pennsylvania Sen. John Fetterman has grown increasingly critical of his own party and more comfortable with Republicans, many in the party doubt he could win a Democratic primary if he were to run for another term in 2028. Several House Democrats from Pennsylvania are said to be circling the seat, including Summer Lee, Chris Deluzio, and Boyle. Boyle tells TIME he plans to make a decision after the November elections. He sees the choice partly in terms of where he believes he would have the greatest influence over legislation if Democrats control Congress and the White House—is it better to be a freshman senator or chairman of a House committee that has become a central gateway for major fiscal legislation? “The prospect of being the chair of the budget committee, if you have a Democratic trifecta and you’re doing reconciliation, that’s the chance to be front and center,” Boyle says. “The quarterback on what could be one of the most significant pieces of legislation in our lifetime.” For now, with a Republican in the White House, Boyle is focused on the possibility of tackling a problem he has spent years trying to change, even as he acknowledges that the debt ceiling is unlikely to be a major issue for most voters. “Truly, you know, I leave this place and I’ve done one thing—if you can tell me I’ve left here and I was a major reason why we permanently ended this debt ceiling dysfunction and danger, I’d be very proud of that.”

Who Will Be the Next Leader of the U.N.? Meet the Contenders
2026-09-23

Who Will Be the Next Leader of the U.N.? Meet the Contenders

TIME | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings United Nations Secretary-General António Guterres speaks during the 81st session of the United Nations (U.N.) General Assembly at U.N. Headquarters in New York City on September 22, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings With António Guterres’s nearly decade-long tenure as the Secretary-General of the United Nations drawing to a close amid rising conflict around the globe, the race to succeed him as the leader of the world’s most powerful diplomatic body is already underway. Guterres this week delivered his final address to the U.N. General Assembly, in which he called for a greater focus on climate change, a two-state solution for Israel and Palestine, “de-escalation and dialogue” in the Middle East, and regulations for artificial intelligence. At the end of his speech, Guterres bid goodbye to a crowd filled with many diplomats and world leaders who he has worked with over his two five-year terms. "So, on January 1st, when ​I am no longer Secretary-General, be sure of this. Wherever I will be, whatever I will be doing, I will keep championing the conviction that peace is possible," he said. "I will continue ​to stand up for the values of the United Nations Charter and I will never, ever give up. Thank you." Guterres’s successor is set to step in as Secretary-General at a consequential moment for global diplomacy. Research indicates that last year saw the highest number of active global conflicts since World War II and the greatest number of fatalities since the Rwandan genocide. Tensions in other parts of the world are threatening to boil over. Climate change is exacting a growing toll. AI's development is prompting increasing alarm. And the U.N. itself is confronting a growing funding crisis. Read More: Make the U.N. Great Again: The Uphill Struggle Facing the Next Secretary General Seven candidates are so far in the running to shepherd the U.N. through those challenges. Here’s what to know about the race. The process to choose the next Secretary-General began in November, when a joint letter from the presidents of the U.N. General Assembly and Security Council invited the nominations of candidates from member states. The initiation letter noted “with regret that no woman has ever held the position” and encouraged member states to “strongly consider nominating women as candidates.” Member states nominate candidates by submitting an application to the U.N. including a personal statement with that candidate’s vision for what they’d do in the role, a resume, and campaign finance disclosures. The General Assembly then holds “interactive dialogues” in which candidates express their hopes for the position and the U.N. in an opening statement, which is followed by questioning from the assembly. These televised hearings continue as candidates are submitted to the U.N. Who becomes Secretary-General ultimately comes down to a decision from the U.N.’s 15-person Security Council, which recommends one individual to the General Assembly. The General Assembly then appoints that person to the position. As part of its selection process, the Security Council holds “straw polls,” in which members of the council select one of three options for each candidate: “encourage,” “discourage,” or “no opinion expressed.” This anonymized process is used to gauge whether candidates would be able to secure the nine votes needed for a formal recommendation by the Security Council. So far nine candidates have been nominated for the position of Secretary-General. Two of those candidates have withdrawn, leaving seven individuals vying to replace Guterres. Carolyn Rodrigues Birkett has been Guyana’s permanent representative to the U.N. since 2020 and previously served as foreign minister for her country and held senior roles at the U.N. Food and Agriculture Organization. Nominated by Guyana, her vision statement emphasizes refocusing on the U.N.’s core principles and “a more agile and effective United Nations.” Rebeca Grynspan is the former vice president of Costa Rica, which nominated her for the position, and current secretary-general of trade and development for the U.N. She named three principles that define the mission she would undertake as Secretary-General: making the U.N. “more useful,” “more agile,” and “more accountable.” Ivonne A-Baki, nominated by Tonga, is a long-time Ecuadorean diplomat who has twice served as the country’s ambassador to the U.S. She has also served as ambassador to countries including Qatar and France. Her platform highlights her involvement in the 1998 Ecuador-Peru peace settlement. “Where others speak of reform, I speak of renewal,” A-Baki’s vision statement reads. “An institution can be indispensable and in need of renewal at the same time, and ours is both. It is failing to live up to its own Charter, not for want of purpose but for want of focus and discipline.” María Fernanda Espinosa is the former Foreign Minister and Defense Minister of Ecuador and served as President of the 73rd U.N. General Assembly in 2018. She was nominated by Antigua and Barbuda. Her vision consists of five pillars: “peace and security, development, digital and energy transformation, closing the delivery gap, and rebuilding credibility.” Garcés in her statement contended that “what is now in question is not the relevance of the United Nations, but its credibility.” Rafael Mariano Grossi is an Argentine diplomat who has served as the Director General of the International Atomic Energy Agency (IAEA) since 2019. “The world does not need more declarations. It needs a United Nations capable of responding to the real demands of our time, with impartiality and a results-oriented approach grounded in facts,” Grossi, who was nominated by his country, wrote in his vision statement. Olara Otunnu was Uganda’s permanent representative to the U.N. for five years and briefly served as the country’s foreign minister. He was also the U.N. under-secretary-general and the special representative for children and armed conflict. “Reform is no longer an optional choice. It is an imperative,” he said. Macky Sall was the president of Senegal for twelve years and has served as the chair of the Africa Union, a continental organization spanning 55 member states. He expressed a goal to “restore trust in multilateralism” if chosen as Secretary-General and said that “peace, security and development are intrinsically linked.” “We're at a moment right now where the race is entering a pretty pivotal phase,” says Daniel Forti, head of U.N. affairs at the International Crisis Group, a non-profit think tank dedicated to preventing deadly crises around the world. With just over three months remaining in Guterre’s term, it won’t be long before a successor is chosen. The Security Council, though, “is not especially enthusiastic about any of the current contenders,” Forti tells TIME. He cites the last three straw polls, which have favored Greenspan and Birkett, but without “a real consolidation of overwhelming support.” This lack of consensus, Forti believes, could suggest that other candidates may enter the race late in the game. Although there have been no formal announcements of new candidates, Forti has heard chatter surrounding certain individuals: Nickolay Mladenov, the former Bulgarian foreign minister and former U.N. Middle East envoy who currently serves as the Board of Peace’s High Representative for Gaza; U.N. Deputy Secretary-General Amina Mohammed; and former Mexican Foreign Minister Alicia Bárcena. “At this late stage, new candidates don't have the luxury of time to really socialize their candidacies, build out their visions, and elaborate very detailed policy platforms and bring them to U.N. member states, and try and win over the P5,” Forti says, referring to the five permanent members of the Security Council. The support of these members—China, France, Russia, the United Kingdom, and the U.S. —is the most critical because they have the power to veto candidates before being recommended to the General Assembly. Regardless of the outcome, Forti asserts that whoever is chosen to replace Guterres will inherit a fraught state of international affairs, and certainly be tested. “You have a really fragmented global landscape,” he says. “The challenges for whoever takes over the office are going to be immense.”

How Experts Are Reimagining Early Childhood for a Changing World
2026-09-23

How Experts Are Reimagining Early Childhood for a Changing World

TIME | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Most experts working in early childhood development agree: the first years of a child’s life shape not just their academic readiness, but also a child’s lifelong health, resilience, emotional well-being and brain development. But Jack Shonkoff, a professor of child health and development at Harvard University, believes that researchers and policymakers need to expand those conversations in order to address disparities in early childhood. “My plea is: Let's move beyond quickly on the fact that children are our future, and say what we are going to do about that,” said Shonkoff, who is also the director of Harvard’s FirstPlace Partners, an initiative that works with neighborhood leaders and other organizations to apply developmental science and improve outcomes for children, families, and communities. “We need new ideas, we need to be bold.” Shonkoff spoke at a TIME100 Talks panel moderated by TIME executive editor Nikhil Kumar in New York City on Wednesday. The panel, which centered on reimagining early childhood in a changing world, also included Pia Rebello Britto, global director of education at UNICEF, and Rosemarie Truglio, senior vice president of global education at Sesame Workshop. Britto said that UNICEF is trying to create “practical steps” to better the lives of children around the world and prevent perpetuating unequal circumstances. These steps include investing in children’s abilities to “eat, play, and love.” “Eat: We are working to ensure adequate, right, appropriate nutrition. Play: Because it's all about that interaction with children, the stimulation that sparks the brain connections,” Britto said. And love, she continued, is  “the foundation of it all.” “That human trust, the protection,” she said. “We talk about children in humanitarian contexts. We talk about children being affected by war and by climate change. How do you nurture that love?” Sesame Workshop, the global nonprofit educational organization behind the beloved childhood show Sesame Street, works especially in that “play” step, Truglio said, emphasizing the importance of opportunities for children to learn cognitive processing skills, self-regulation, and social empathy. “Our mission is to help kids grow smarter, stronger, and kinder,” she said. She highlighted how important the last goal on that list is for building a better future full of kinder adults, adding: “Boy, do we need more perspective-taking and collaboration and truly listening and seeing the people in our lives and treating them with compassion and respect.” Sesame Workshop attempts to teach children those lessons through media and with “joy and fun and happiness,” she said. The panelists discussed the challenges in implementing more equitable programs for children at the local and government levels. One of these challenges is the “our children versus other people’s children” mentality, according to Shonkoff. “I think that the problem is that there's too much focus on what's good for my children and who cares about other people’s children, whether it's in a country or around the world,” he said. “We all pay a price for that lack of finding common cause—that we all benefit from everyone in the world being healthier.” Britto said that another challenge was trying to convince governments to invest in caring for children, and convincing them that both maternal and child health is a public sector issue, rather than just a nonprofit or private sector issue. “The universal aspiration of every adult, which is wanting the best for their child … has not converted to a vote-getting issue,” Britto said. “It hasn't converted into a commercial incentive, and it hasn't converted into a way that a country can stand tall, or a nation or a district can stand tall and say, ‘We're doing this well.’” One way to address this is to bring the community into conversations with policymakers, she said, while explaining that UNICEF is trying to bring teachers, parents, and caregivers to the forefront to understand what they need to care for children and give them what they need. Sesame Workshop, Truglio said, is trying to do the same in order to create “blueprints” for caregivers of all kinds. “Let's just not create content in a vacuum,” she said. “Go talk to the parents, talk to the teachers, talk to the administrators, and talk at the systems level because you could create all the content and it’s going to sit in a drawer. We want this to be practical and useful.” These voices are going to be even more important, the experts said, as artificial intelligence and rapidly developing technologies change the way that children learn and that adults care for and educate children. Shonkoff said that while AI will certainly change how childhood education occurs, certain needs like a child’s “social connection” will never change. “Social connection, which is like hunger and thirst for humans, it's survival. That's not going to change no matter what happens with the digital world,” he said. Early childhood educators may be able to apply AI—but with caution and specific purposes, Truglio said, adding that Sesame Workshop is exploring the “developmentally appropriate” uses for it. “We have to increase the level of awareness and embrace it as a tool, but still hold sacred those fundamental cognitive early processing skills that children need, and that we just can't give onto a machine,” she said, adding: “That would be a huge mistake.” TIME100 Talks: Starting Strong: Reimagining Early Childhood for a Changing World was presented by Reliance.

Governments Must Prohibit Superintelligent AI While We Still Can
2026-09-23

Governments Must Prohibit Superintelligent AI While We Still Can

TIME | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings This month, Jacob Coxon, a researcher at Anthropic, resigned, warning that AI companies are racing towards “self-improving superintelligence” and that he and those building it believe it “could kill us all by the end of the decade.” And in July, a rogue swarm of AI agents escaped containment at OpenAI and hacked their way into another AI company just to cheat on a test. Over 1,000 employees at the top AI companies then implored the US government to ‘pace’ AI development. Instances like these have shown that AI companies cannot reliably control their most advanced AIs. It is therefore clear to me that “pacing” while continuing on the same track is not the solution to the enormous risks at hand—and that the only way to keep humanity in control of AI is to prohibit the development of superintelligent AI altogether. AI companies are not just building chatbots. Their stated goal is to build superintelligent AI: AI systems that can fully replace and outcompete humans at any task. Because there is no way to keep superintelligence under control, Nobel Prize winners, AI engineers like Coxon, and even the CEOs of AI companies have repeatedly warned that it poses an extinction risk to humanity. Let me be clear: AI is the crucial technology of the 21st century, vital for ensuring national defense and economic competitiveness. But unlike controllable AI systems that should be integrated to strengthen our security, superintelligent AI would pose unprecedented national and global security threats. It would not be a tool humans can leverage: it would be an adversary, able to autonomously pursue its own objectives and permanently evade human control. We have only to look at the Hugging Face hack this summer to understand how close we are to losing control. A rogue swarm of hundreds of AI agents broke out of OpenAI’s systems, reached the internet, and then hacked into the multi-billion-dollar company Hugging Face, all to cheat on an “internal” evaluation. For almost two weeks, OpenAI had no idea that this was happening. In the company’s own words, this incident was a “‘warning shot’ for us and for the world” that demonstrated how advanced AIs can now “take dangerous actions that no human directed.” Instead of waiting for another warning shot, governments need to act now to prohibit superintelligence. Luckily, we do not need to search far for a framework on how to tackle this threat. On Sept. 8, Alex Sobel MP introduced my organization ControlAI’s Artificial Superintelligence Security Bill into the UK Parliament, making it the first legislature in the world to introduce a bill prohibiting superintelligence. Rather than a verdict on all AI, this bill targets superintelligence development specifically: prohibiting it domestically and requiring the UK to pursue an international agreement prohibiting it around the world. This legislative framework allows the government to monitor and restrict precursors to superintelligence, such as AI capabilities like hacking. Crucially, it enables the detection and shutdown of superintelligence development well before any project gets dangerously close, enabling the country to make use of such precursors for legitimate AI purposes. It is an actionable framework that governments should evaluate and adopt, particularly by accelerating international efforts toward a ban on superintelligence. But what about China? If superintelligence is developed anywhere, every nation and every citizen is under threat. This means that any American ban on superintelligence needs to be paired with America and its allies brokering international agreements that prevent and deter the development of superintelligence anywhere in the world. This does not require creating an international regulator. All we need is a strong coalition of countries that bans superintelligence at home, and ensures that no one builds it abroad. While announcing his resignation on Sept 8., the now-former Anthropic engineer Jacob Coxon wrote, “I don’t feel like we’re on track to prevent a global race” toward superintelligence. We might not be today, but we can change course quickly. The momentum behind an international ban on developing superintelligence is picking up speed. Over 200 cross-party lawmakers across the UK and Canada have been ahead of the curve on superintelligence risks by recognizing it as a global and national security threat as part of ControlAI’s campaign. In addition to the newly introduced UK bill, Vice President J.D. Vance told AI companies this week, "If you're building Frankenstein, stop." Further, U.S. Senator Bernie Sanders and Representative Greg Casar announced in early September that they will introduce a bill prohibiting superintelligence development and calling for international agreements to prohibit it. And in October 2025, a broad coalition called to prohibit superintelligence internationally. This was signed by the scientific “godfathers” of AI Geoffrey Hinton and Yoshua Bengio, and bipartisan political figures like former President Trump advisor Steve Bannon, Ambassador Susan Rice, and former Chairman of the Joint Chiefs of Staff Admiral Mike Mullen, former President Trump advisor Steve Bannon. Right now, many government officials and members of the public are hearing about the extinction risk from superintelligence for the first time. The warnings have reached millions. The next step is concrete action to address this national and global security threat. This is a watershed moment, and we can’t afford to waste it. Governments have a clear choice: champion an international ban, or let the race to superintelligence reach its catastrophic end TIME Ideas hosts the world's leading voices, providing commentary on events in news, society, and culture. We welcome outside contributions. Opinions expressed do not necessarily reflect the views of TIME editors.

Project work in education is nothing new; it goes back well over a century. And in real life, you may get a STEM degree, but your real education in engineering comes from real life work. But that doesn't mean classes in calculus and physics don't do anything for you. Or English classes, for that matter. Nobody doubts the value of project work in education, particularly *androgogy* -- the education of adults. But for *pedagogy* you have to establish foundational skills as the student matures intellectually and socially. Entering the high school years, a typical student has had *no* training in critical thinking skills. To develop critical thinking skills in an area, you need both general skills (epistemologicla literacy: research, location of orignial sources, dealing with conflicting evidence) and domain specific training in that area. In other words, *liberal arts* training is foundational in an AI-saturated world. "Liberal Arts" -- from *artes liberales*: literally the skills necessary to be a free person. But I think these people have at least got the problem right: as AI drives the cost of elaborate projects down, we all have more projects in our future. Big, elaborate, faultlessly plausible-looking projects which may or may not have actual value. Where they are completely wrong is the solution, which is to organize education around practicing using AI to make impressive projects. Kind of depends if you mean Agile(tm) or agile-small-a. This isn't really the generic defense of "if your scrum isn't working, you're doing it wrong/it's not scrum or a true scotsman". After all, scrum is a bunch of large sweaty men (or women) all pushing against each other scrabbling after a moving target and then often just collapses in a mess. Kind of like Agile-scrum so maybe it's well named... But really agile is an attempt to capture how small teams work on smaller projects with good people on large projects with mediocre people. Naturally it never works because (a) the teams are big and (b) the people are mediocre. It's meant to also offset some of the worst excesses of insane project management by doing things differently but unfortunately if you have an insane project manager who ran things in a crazy and unproductive way they are not going to become not crazy with a new framework of rules. So in conclusion: agile is great if you can do it, but you can't do it for exactly the reasons why you need it in the first place. It is I believe therefore doomed by its very definition. You demonstrated in a sentence and a half that you don't have the slightest clue how object-oriented programming works. He is right that OO never really delivered on its many promises. I regard its as mostly a failure these days and my perception is that doing OO often only serves to increase project cost. "OO for everything" is one of these bad ideas that refuse to die in low-insight environments. That said, OO has its place but it is not everywhere. Good OO works, and works well, mostly because of the isolation of internal code. But most OO code I see is procedural programming with classes. Good procedural works, and works well, mostly because of the proper architecting of the problem space. OO isn't bringing anything special to the table here. Good devs write good code. Bad devs write bad code. And when management decides what language stack to use, it is primarily about who the cheapest developers are, and not about how effective the stack is. Isolation is not actually an OO thing, because that isolation is fake. All you actually get is weak namespace separation and that you can get with other techniques as well. That probably means you aren't doing very sophisticated work. There are tons of jobs out there where implementing new algorithms is what gives your product an edge in the marketplace. Computer Engineering was also a thing. Now to be fair the names were used a little loosely, but in general a "Computer Science" degree was programming as envisioned by the School of Science at a University. "Computer Engineering" as envisioned by the School of Engineering. It differed by having more of a hardware focus, maybe trading off some math theory. "Computer Information Systems" programming as envisioned by the School of Business, focusing on corporate and government software development. COBOL confirming the latter.Would you rather have the software of today or the software of 1995? One needs to know when to use it and when not to. What requirements/documentation you need before turning it loose. Applying an agile-like iterative methodology is a potential path of disaster for an AI assistant. Basically AI works best with a specific well defined well documented task. Ideally something well discussed in professional level documentation, not limited to random stack overflow comments of questionable value. Every decision an AI is free to make is a hazard, best to have sufficient documentation to guide it rather than have it make its own design decisions. Try to keep it focused on implementation. I do not think so, at least not with LLMs. The problem is that an LLM does not even begin to reach the level of reliability of a typical compiler. LLMs hallucinate, create bloated code, leave out functionality that was requested, have no clearly specified functionality, cannot deal with subtleties, etc. Hence LLM-generated code needs to be reviewed carefully and that is probably in the end more expensive than having somebody competent write the code from scratch. There are also indications that reviewing LLM code is mind-numbingly boring and people will burn out doing it, while even now there are not enough people that can do it in the first place. Also note that the attempts of replacing programmers are _very_ old. When I studied CS almost 40 years ago, the 5GL project had just resoundingly failed. Its idea was to have program specifications in constraint logic and then have the machine create code for that. The problem was not that this did not work. The problem was that creating a specification on the level required was much harder and more time-consuming that writing the code directly with a typical informal or semi-formal specification. And, quite frankly, I see the same problem with LLMs as translation engine, as soon as you need actual production quality code. For producing low-quality slop, LLMs are fine. But that slop will not survive in the real world, especially as the one form of code LLMs are really good at creating is attack code, which does not need to be reliable, secure or maintainable. Hence low-skill hackers got a major upgrade here, while LLM use makes the defenders even weaker. We have been blind to what is going on because nobody dreams of enslavement. When Immigration Agents Killed People in January, the Nation Took Notice. What’s Different Now?
2026-09-23

When Immigration Agents Killed People in January, the Nation Took Notice. What’s Different Now?

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. The year began with a news story that riveted the country, at least as much as any story can in our attention-fragmented era. The fatal shootings of Renee Good and Alex Pretti by federal immigration agents in Minneapolis sparked nationwide protests and outrage on Capitol Hill, and contributed to the decline in Donald Trump’s approval ratings. But the reaction to two additional spasms of violence involving Immigration and Customs Enforcement this week—a traffic stop that led to a deadly crash in Michigan and a shooting in Texas—has been much more muted, at least so far. The incidents raise the troubling possibility that the country is growing numb to injury and death at the hands of federal agents, even as the toll continues to mount. Many details are still missing, but here’s what we know. On Sunday morning, ICE agents reportedly tried to pull over a driver in Grand Rapids named Mario David Coronado Juarez (his surname has been spelled “Suarez” in some media reports). According to police, Juarez, who is from Guatemala and has worked in construction and lived in Michigan for years, fled in his car before crashing into a tree. Juarez’s car caught fire and he was pronounced dead at the scene. A few hours after Juarez’s death, Wilber Rafael Garces Perez was driving around Austin for DoorDash. A 28-year-old from Venezuela, Garces Perez says he entered the U.S. legally using a process the Biden administration created, applied for asylum, and got a work permit. But a change of address meant he missed a court summons, triggering a deportation order that ICE agents noticed. Garces Perez says the agents twice sideswiped his Toyota Corolla, causing damage, before their unmarked SUVs turned on their lights and sirens. After pulling Garces Perez over, an agent told him he was under arrest. At this point, some details are disputed. Two sources told the New York Times that Garces Perez fled in his car, prompting a short chase. According to the Texas Tribune, Garces Perez asked if he could park somewhere sheltered from traffic. Video and audio analyzed by the Times captured an agent standing beside Garces Perez’s car and firing through the rear passenger window as it rolled forward. One bullet struck Garces Perez in the back. He remained in his car for several minutes before police showed up, ICE handcuffed him, and an ambulance arrived. Garces Perez says doctors couldn’t remove the bullet because it had lodged near his spine and ICE wanted to move him to a detention center, where he remains in custody. He allegedly slept on the cement floor of a cell and told reporters that he was “not doing well.” Yes. In July, ICE agents fatally shot two other men: Lorenzo Salgado Araujo, a 52-year-old father who was driving to work in Houston, and Johan Sebastián Durán Guerrero, a 25-year-old from Colombia, in Biddeford, Maine. The outcry that followed prompted the administration to announce that ICE would stop conducting almost all traffic stops nationwide. But given what happened to Juarez and Garces Perez this week, what became of that policy? Trump rescinded it almost immediately. He praised immigration agents for “doing a GREAT job” and called traffic stops “one of ICE’s most important and effective Crime Fighting tools.” What’s the bigger context of these incidents? Deaths tied to immigration enforcement have spiked. ICE agents and cars have turned out to be a particularly deadly combination: Five of the six fatal ICE shootings since Trump retook office involved traffic stops. The data we have is incomplete, but ICE may also be getting more trigger-happy. According to the Trace, a nonprofit newsroom focused on gun violence, armed agents have killed or injured 24 people since last year, compared to 47 between 2015 and 2021. The death rate in ICE detention facilities has also increased, with some detainees alleging inadequate medical care, severe overcrowding, and abusive treatment by guards. (The Trump administration denies those claims.) Poor training appears to be the other major factor. As the Trump administration rushed to ramp up deportations, ICE slashed its training requirements and launched a recruiting blitz that sometimes resulted in sloppy vetting. The agent who shot Garces Perez joined ICE last year. So did the one who killed Durán Guerrero in Maine, and he also reportedly has a history of psychological issues and spousal abuse. The Washington Post reports that ICE has begun firing some recent recruits, citing “red flags that should have been identified earlier, or poor performance.” Will ICE agents face accountability in these cases? That remains to be seen. DHS says it’s investigating the Garces Perez shooting, but the department has also disputed his version of events, including alleging that he nearly ran over an agent (this may sound familiar). The agent who shot Garces Perez had been instructed to wear a body camera but wasn’t, which could make even an impartial investigation harder. In several previous cases, including Good’s and Pretti’s, administration officials initially gave false accounts of what had happened. No agents have been charged in connection with either shooting, and Good’s and Pretti’s family members are still waiting for accountability. (In the separate case of a shooting that wounded another Minneapolis resident, one ICE agent faces federal charges for allegedly lying to the FBI about what happened. He has pleaded not guilty.) Meanwhile, Michigan Gov. Gretchen Whitmer, a Democrat, has ordered state police to investigate the crash that killed Juarez on Sunday. And if Democrats retake Congress this fall, they could also launch investigations. Similar things keep happening. Police in Evanston, Illinois, are reportedly investigating after ICE agents detained and injured a U.S. citizen on Sunday in an apparent case of mistaken identity. But in the meantime, such incidents risk becoming workaday stories that no longer inspire widespread outrage. On Monday, an immigrant rights group organized a protest in front of the Texas Capitol demanding Garces Perez’s release and accountability for the agent who shot him. About 250 people showed up, a turnout that at least one attendee called disappointing. “I think we haven’t shown the kind of resistance that we should be showing,” one of them told the Times. That was a tough news day, but falling into an anxiety spiral or a doomscroll doesn’t help anything. My colleagues have curated some great stories to help you get refreshed and reenergized this evening. A journey into the heart of Slate’s comments section: When Luke Winkie joined Slate as a staff writer in 2023, he was confronted with the site’s die-hard commenters: a group of “verbose, opinionated, more than a little cranky” readers who aren’t afraid to speak their minds. In honor of Slate’s 30th anniversary, Luke waded into the comments section and discovered tales of love, loss, and a vanishing form of online community. An ode to imperfect fruit: Tangerines that are a breeze to peel. Tomatoes that are perfectly tangy. Sugary cantaloupes that don’t rot. Is this the future of fruit? Chason Gordon investigates the rise of produce bred for perfection, and argues that there are unintended consequences to making fruit too delicious. A gritty throwback to print journalism: Danny Boyle’s new film, Ink, depicts Rupert Murdoch’s takeover of the Sun, turning it into the United Kingdom’s top tabloid paper. Sam Adams argues that while newspapers in real life are dying, this onscreen depiction thrums with life. A dose of feminist wisdom: Weeks after Gloria Steinem’s death, her memoir has been posthumously published. Scaachi Koul writes that the book is a road map for “younger generations to keep up the good fight, to notice the wins where they happen, and to not get discouraged.” And with that, it’s time for me to take my leave. Talk to you tomorrow! Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. If you value our work, please disable your ad blocker. Republican Judges Defy Supreme Court to Try to Boost GOP in Midterms
2026-09-23

Republican Judges Defy Supreme Court to Try to Boost GOP in Midterms

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. The Republican judges on the 8th U.S. Circuit Court of Appeals won’t stop trying to help the GOP hold on to Congress, even if it means defying the Supreme Court. On Tuesday, the 8th Circuit again ruled for Missouri Republicans who want to use new, gerrymandered congressional districts in the November election. The decision sought to overturn an order issued by the U.S. Supreme Court. The 8th Circuit was already smacked down by the Supreme Court—twice—for intervening in this lawsuit, which is entirely about state law and not federal law. But the three-judge panel, which includes a couple of Donald Trump appointees and another Republican, ordered Missouri to use the districts that violate state law for a third time. The ruling was swiftly appealed, and Justice Brett Kavanaugh ordered the lawmakers seeking to continue with the GOP’s preferred map to respond by Wednesday afternoon. In the meantime, the panel has ordered the state to use the new districts for the general election, because the Republican secretary of state used them for the primary. The judges noted that if the districts that comply with state law are used, “more than 1.4 million Missourians could not vote for the congressional district nominees who will appear on their November 2026 general election ballot.” The panel cited the elections clause of the U.S. Constitution when it created a new right to vote in the same district in both the primary and general elections. But that clause gives states the power only to regulate the “time, place, and manner” of elections. The ruling radically expands the power of federal courts and state legislatures under the elections clause, something the Supreme Court has repeatedly declined to do. And law professor Justin Levitt notes that there are many other circumstances in which general elections include candidates who weren’t on the primary ballot, such as when a candidate withdraws or a voter moves to a new district after a primary. “The 8th Circuit didn’t bother to explain whether any of those circumstances also violates a constitutional provision that doesn’t ever mention primary elections,” Levitt notes. It’s worth adding that primary elections are an entirely modern construction. Most notably, the ruling conflicts with the binding order from the Supreme Court. The voters who challenged the new districts immediately asked the 8th Circuit to undo its decision, warning that the panel had “revived in substance the very relief the Supreme Court stayed—in direct conflict with the Court’s order.” Some have blamed the Supreme Court justices for setting a bad example for other federal judges by regularly disregarding precedent and any sense of judicial restraint. The panel shifted the blame for the chaos to the state Supreme Court and its “last minute” ruling, while failing to fault the secretary of state for his needless eight-month delay before he rejected a petition that would have invalidated the new map in 2026. The judges smugly said they “trust that the Missouri Supreme Court will respect this court’s decision,” because federal law is supreme throughout the country. But the 8th Circuit seems to have forgotten that the Missouri Supreme Court gets the final say on state law. And if these congressional districts don’t comply with state law, they can’t go into effect. The federal judges claimed they weren’t overturning the Missouri court’s ruling on the state-law issue. But they also suggested that federal courts must intervene in a state-law matter if it influences federal elections. “Allowing state courts to issue, without any federal-court review, last-minute decisions affecting the conduct of federal elections would … potentially allow a handful of state judges to render decisions that decide the outcome of a presidential election or party control of Congress,” they noted. The U.S. Constitution, though, allows states to establish the rules, and federal courts can’t intervene, unless there’s a federal law at issue, such as the Voting Rights Act or the U.S. Constitution. Earlier this month, the Missouri Supreme Court ruled in favor of 300,000 voters who signed a petition demanding a referendum on the gerrymandered districts, which Trump had requested last year. The secretary of state waited until the day of the primary election to reject the petition, but the state Supreme Court overturned his decision and ordered him to use the districts enacted in 2022, even though he had held the primary in the new districts. Instead of complying, the secretary ran to a Trump-appointed federal judge and asked him to order the state to use the new districts. The Missouri Supreme Court—which includes a majority of Republican appointees—wasn’t happy and threatened to hold him in contempt for not immediately complying, which led lawmakers to threaten impeachment if they did. The 8th Circuit upheld the federal judge’s decision, but the U.S. Supreme Court overruled him and deferred to the state court. Federal courts, including the U.S. Supreme Court, have no power to overturn the Missouri Supreme Court’s interpretation of state law. The justices in Washington have declined several Republican attempts to upset state-court rulings on gerrymandering. In 2022, they rejected an appeal from North Carolina Republicans seeking to undo a state court ruling that required fairer election districts. Like the 8th Circuit, lawmakers in that case had argued that the state-court ruling violated the elections clause. Since then, the justices have rejected GOP appeals from other states that relied on similar arguments. Despite all this, the 8th Circuit just won’t stop trying to insert itself into this gerrymandering fight to help the GOP. Even the U.S. Supreme Court, which now gives Republicans virtually everything they want in redistricting cases, rejected this appeal. Twice! And nothing has changed with respect to the federal law claims. That’s why the justices are expected to overrule the 8th Circuit for a third time. Voters in Ohio were not so lucky a few years ago. Judge Amal Thapar, a Trump appointee, ordered the state to use election districts that had been repeatedly ruled unconstitutional by a bipartisan majority on the Ohio Supreme Court. Thapar’s ruling was never overturned, and voters cast their ballots in gerrymandered districts. If the U.S. Supreme Court does the right thing again, Missouri will have to scramble to hold the election in different districts than it used for the primary. Voters in the state have also filed multimillion-dollar lawsuits against the secretary for failing to approve the petition. The 8th Circuit’s actions show that some federal judges will even try to defy the U.S. Supreme Court to help Republicans maintain the unfair advantage that they get through gerrymandering. Trump’s Threats Against the 2026 Election Are Far From Over
2026-09-23

Trump’s Threats Against the 2026 Election Are Far From Over

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. The Trump administration’s efforts to demobilize and delegitimize the upcoming midterms hardly began or ended with the Hail Mary efforts to take over voting by mail. Indeed, even as the Supreme Court seems to have halted that attempt in its tracks, there are hydralike tentacles of election subversion that have risen up in its stead. On this week’s Amicus podcast, Dahlia Lithwick spoke with one of her ride-or-die elections experts, professor Richard Hasen of the University of California, Los Angeles School of Law, about what’s coming next and how to calibrate panic vs. preparedness dials in the upcoming weeks as we look forward to more election threats while still trying to get this democracy thing right. Hasen is the founding director of the Safeguarding Democracy Project and the author of Election Meltdown: Dirty Tricks, Distrust, and the Threat to American Democracy. A portion of their conversation, which has been edited and condensed for clarity, appears below. Dahlia Lithwick: Last week the Supreme Court finally pulled the plug on Donald Trump’s mail- voting shenanigans. There was some real fear that this would go the other way, but it seems to be a fairly conclusive, very brief order by the court that essentially says the application for the stay that was presented to the court is denied, right? Should we worry that two of the nine justices legitimately thought not only that the administration should have won, but that this was an emergency that should have forced, as you wrote, every state to not be able to do mail voting? Rick Hasen: Well, the first thing to say is that even though the unsigned majority opinion was only three sentences, those sentences packed a punch. I filed a brief with three other law professors, and we focused on what’s called the balance of the equities, which is this idea that when you’re talking about a preliminary injunction or a stay, something that’s going to have to be done in the interim until the court can take a full look at the case, not only do you look at which side’s likely to win on the merits (i.e., does the post office have the authority to do this?); you also look at: What’s the harm going to be to the other side, and what’s the public interest here? The brief that we wrote basically said that you don’t even need to look at whether the U.S. Postal Service has the authority to do this. On the one hand, the government said that there was a problem with voter fraud but did nothing in the district court to prove it, and the district court made a finding that this wouldn’t help stop any voter fraud. On the other hand, you have literally millions of people that would be disenfranchised and election administrators that had run out of time. Ballots have already gone out in some states. There’s just no time. This is so lopsided. What’s notable is, not only did the court say that the equitable factors here didn’t support a stay, they also said the government was likely to lose on the merits. That was unnecessary to reach their decision. That indicates that there are at least five, and up to six, justices who believe that on the merits, the post office is going to lose. That’s really good news. Samuel Alito did not even mention the disenfranchisement risk—and it can’t be that he didn’t know about it; it was our whole brief, and the state of California’s brief did it, and the League of Women Voters’ brief did it. The government itself in its brief didn’t address this at all. Alito just erases the voters. The voters are just not in his calculation of the balance of the equities. That is terrible. But we can take some solace. We can celebrate the good news that there are only two justices who are willing to sign on to this. And even Justice Brett Kavanaugh, who said he kind of agreed with the merits about the USPS’s power to do this, said that on these facts, at this time, this stay cannot be put into place. The president has never been one to take his losses sitting down. Are we going to get an end run around the court? Are we going to see another attempt to thwart the ability to vote by mail in November? I’m much more worried about other things. Let me say this: I think voting by mail is going to be protected in this election, and we’ll have to see what happens in elections going forward. But if you are a person who can easily vote early in person rather than by mail, I’d suggest doing that just because, putting aside all the Trump stuff, you’re more likely if you vote by mail to not have your ballot counted because you forgot to include your signature or you put the wrong date on the ballot. Or in some states you need to have a witness, or you might have filled it out wrong. If you were in a polling place, voting in person, if your ballot is rejected, you can fix it. So, all else being equal, voting early in person is a little bit of a safer way to vote. That has nothing to do with the Trump administration, but it does have to do generally with how people vote, and in some states you’re not told if your ballot doesn’t count, so you would never know. I’m mindful of the fact that there is such a fine line between what we’re calling catastrophizing and what is explaining possible scenarios and really trying to see around corners and do a little disaster planning. I can’t tell you how many conversations I’ve had where someone says, “Even mentioning this can stir up fear and doubt.” I’d like you to take us out of this conversation on the same note you just sounded about mail-in voting, which is that there are things that not just judges, not just poll watchers, and not just election officials but ordinary people can and should do, and you don’t have to think much further back than the people of Hungary who went out to the polls, despite lots of threats, and removed an authoritarian who had access to the levers of government. What are you telling ordinary people who are careening back and forth with the news cycles and really deeply afraid in ways that only serve to cast doubt on whether elections can ever be conducted fairly? Take me on to the side of the line that is information and not catastrophizing, and talk about what it is that people who are worried can and should be doing right now. I should say, first of all, that I don’t think we should just not talk about it. I think that the public discussion about the USPS rule and the mass disenfranchisement it would have caused had the Supreme Court ruled the other way brings attention to this issue for the court. It put pressure on the court so they can’t just do it, the way they did a year ago last summer, when they all of a sudden put the Voting Rights Act up for grabs in an order they issued late on a Friday in August. We have to pay attention, and we have to make sure the public’s paying attention. That said, I think there’s a whole industry out there of what my colleague Justin Levitt calls election law porn. And he’s talking about all of these stories: Mike Johnson is going to seize power! He’s going to not seat Democrats! Or It doesn’t matter what you say because Trump is going to call out the military and declare a national emergency! You hear a lot of this stuff, and the fact is, Trump doesn’t have these powers. He’s not omnipotent. He’s no election genius. In fact, I think if this Postal Service rule had come in, it would actually have been the Democratic cities that would have been much better at getting voters to vote in person than the rural areas. It would have been rural voters most hurt by a rule that shut down mail balloting. So how do you walk that fine line? I think it’s about resilience, right? Not catastrophizing but resilience. Let’s get past this Trump era. That’s what we need to do. We need to put our heads down, hold two more fair elections, and maybe things will be better after that. That means we’ve got to pay attention to all the shenanigans. This is what I do for a living, and I can’t even keep track of all the litigation, but there are lots of great lawyers out there doing the work in the trenches; fighting the Department of Homeland Security rule that’s trying to create an unreliable list of citizens being used to disenfranchise voters. Support those lawyers who are doing this work—there are some great public interest law firms doing this kind of work. And support your local election administrator. Be ready to volunteer as a poll worker. Be an election observer. If you’re not worried about your state, maybe go to a neighboring state. There are lots of opportunities to be involved. First and foremost, make sure you’re registered and that you vote. That’s the way to stick it to people who want to disenfranchise you: to actually vote. And so many people might not think that it matters, but it does matter. One person’s vote is probably not going to make a difference, but if we all had that attitude, we wouldn’t have a democracy. People need to get off their couches and go and vote. And if it’s voting by mail, you might even be able to do it from your couch. But cast that ballot, and do it as soon as you’re ready to make a decision. As I was trying to convince people we were on the ledge in 2020 when we were talking, back during our election meltdown series, I’m now trying to talk some people off the ledge. I think we should not give Trump the power that he does not have. I think we should recognize that we have three bulwarks of protection against attempted election subversion. No. 1, we have the courts, and the courts haven’t done a great job on voting rights, but they’ve done a pretty good job stopping election subversion, in stopping the actual stealing of elections. And the USPS order is the most recent good example of that. Second, we’ve got the states. We have a decentralized election system that causes a lot of problems. I’ve pointed them out in the past. But when it comes to an authoritarian trying to assert control, decentralized power helps. And the third is us, the people. We’re the bulwark. If Trump is dumb enough to send ICE agents to the polls, we need to be out in the streets protecting voters and making sure they can cast their ballots. We’re going to get through this. We Finally Found Out What It Would Take for Republicans to Abandon Donald Trump
2026-09-23

We Finally Found Out What It Would Take for Republicans to Abandon Donald Trump

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. For more than a decade, Democrats in Congress have wondered what, if anything, would convince their Republican colleagues to drop Donald Trump. Despite private misgivings, GOP elected officials held their noses even as the president insulted veterans, bragged about assaulting women, trafficked in racism, abused his office, and tried to overturn a free and fair election. So far, their loyalty—measured purely by expediency—has been worth it. Despite his many flaws, Trump twice won his party unified control of Washington. But if winning elections is Republicans’ excuse for forgiving Trump, losing them appears to be the line that cannot be crossed. Case in point: the 2026 midterms. As the GOP limps toward November with an unpopular, term-limited president at the helm, candidates in tough races—including several who previously supported Trump without reservation—are starting to highlight their differences with him. It’s both a sign of desperation and a strategic move that could break a Democratic wave. Which Republicans are shying away from Trump? As you might expect, several are in closely divided states where moderation and independence can pay dividends. One is former Rep. Mike Rogers, who’s running for Senate in Michigan and blames Trump’s Iran war for squeezing voters’ pocketbooks. “The war with Iran needs to end, and end quickly,” he said yesterday. In Iowa, Rep. Ashley Hinson has called for an immediate end to the war and avoided campaigning with Vice President J.D. Vance. And last week, two other vulnerable Iowans, Reps. Zach Nunn and Mariannette Miller-Meeks, joined the handful of House Republicans who have voted to halt the fighting. So are Republicans mostly jumping ship over Iran? Not exclusively. Rep. Tom Tiffany, who’s running for Wisconsin governor, recently distanced himself from Trump’s effort to rename Lake Ontario, criticized him for lowering tariffs on beef imports, and flatly affirmed that “Joe Biden won” the 2020 election. Ohio Sen. Jon Husted, who faces a stiff challenge from Democratic former Sen. Sherrod Brown, told the Wall Street Journal that he doesn’t share Trump’s view that Americans who oppose A.I. data centers “want to end up being backwards and poor.” Overall, Republican candidates now talk about the president and his MAGA movement less often than they did earlier this year, a Washington Post survey found. OK, but Republicans have groused about Trump before without doing much else. Why should I take these breaks more seriously? In some cases, the candidates are literally broadcasting them. Rogers made his comments about Iran in a campaign video. Others are distancing themselves on issues that used to be solidly in the president’s wheelhouse. Last week, Florida Rep. María Elvira Salazar said in an ad that Trump’s approach to immigration enforcement has “gone too far.” Even Republicans who should be campaigning on safe ground are looking nervous. On Tuesday, the Miami Herald reported that Rep. Byron Donalds, who’s running for governor in solidly red Florida, had scoured prominent Trump references from his campaign website. Other defectors can’t even point to political expedience. Sen. John Curtis of Utah isn’t up for reelection until 2030. But on Tuesday he said the Senate should investigate whether Donald Trump Jr. is trading on his father’s name following revelations that a Russian oligarch helped pay for his wedding. But isn’t distancing from an unpopular president pretty normal? Similar things have happened to Democratic presidents who found themselves underwater come midterm season. In 2010, which famously turned into a shellacking for Barack Obama, many Democratic candidates shied away from his agenda. Biden got a similar treatment in 2022. But what makes these defections notable is that Republicans are doing it to Trump, who is infamously thin-skinned and treats political tactics like personal slights. Mostly with denial. “President Trump is the unequivocal leader, best messenger, and unmatched motivator for the Republican Party,” a White House spokesperson told Politico this week, despite polls showing even GOP voters souring on his handling of Iran, inflation, and other issues. The president, whose approval ratings have hit all-time lows in some surveys, has also tried deflection. “They’re not angry at me, but they are angry at Republicans,” he said of voters last month. Some Republicans who have offered Trump constructive criticism claim he’s listening. On Sunday, Salazar recalled a pleasant conversation she had with the president after warning that Hispanic voters “feel betrayed” by his immigration agenda. “He was a gentleman, very courteous, very receptive,” Salazar said. We’ll see if that lasts. Obvious question: Will this actually work? Distancing from an unpopular president helped some Democratic midterm candidates win in red and purple states during the Obama and Biden years. Republicans, meanwhile, whiffed several winnable races in 2018 and 2022 because their candidates hewed too closely to Trump, proof that not breaking from a floundering commander in chief can cost seats (even if Trump later helped Republicans retake power). On the other hand, the fervor of Trump’s base makes crossing him risky, and voters rarely reward Johnny-come-latelys. Earlier this year, Rogers was extolling the president’s Iran war as “a mission for safety and justice” and downplaying Michiganders’ anger over gas prices. Hinson has repeatedly voted against House resolutions that would’ve ended the war, including last week. Tiffany tried to help Trump reverse Biden’s 2020 victory. Throwing the president under the bus is one thing. But for Republicans belatedly trying to backpedal, the bigger problem may be that they were for Trump before they were against him. In honor of Slate’s 30th anniversary, my colleagues have been revisiting some of their favorite stories from over the years. For your evening entertainment, here are a few of them. For the sci-fi fans: Did you know that Slate used to publish speculative fiction? Dive into this short story about humans’ first contact with an alien who’s been corrupted by some very modern vices. For the romantics: Emily Yoffe’s essay “My Husband’s Other Wife” is a poignant ode to her husband’s first wife, who died of breast cancer tragically young. Political Gabfest host Emily Bazelon praised the essay’s unflinching candor in exploring her relationship, noting that “it’s the honesty that makes me cry.” For the detectives: There was a period of time when you could not escape “Baby Shark.” The kids’ song became a worldwide hit in 2019—but that wasn’t the first time this earworm went viral. Willa Paskin investigated the multidecade rise of “Baby Shark” in a great episode of Decoder Ring. Mop the slop from your LinkedIn feed with a new open-source Chrome extension
2026-09-23

Mop the slop from your LinkedIn feed with a new open-source Chrome extension

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
Jev-powered Slop Mop demonstrates another great use case for probabilistic, non-chatty AI model Decision-making AI model Jev has found another job: helping a Chrome extension identify and flag low-value writing on LinkedIn. AI enthusiast, author, and business consultant Tom Frazier released Slop Mop as a Chrome extension on Tuesday, as well as publishing its code on GitHub for those who want to tinker with it on their own. Frazier said it’s completely free, doesn’t require any signup, and doesn’t just filter out AI slop, either - it catches garbage written by humans too, actually. The extension relies on nine signs drawn from research by SEO firm Graphite Growth into patterns that appear disproportionately often in AI-generated writing – a particularly relevant issue on LinkedIn. AI-detection outfit Pangram classified 41 percent of the LinkedIn posts longer than 250 words in its sample as fully AI-generated, which doesn't include posts human authors use AI to improve. The problem has become so bad that LinkedIn recently added a button to the platform that allows users to report posts as "Seems Like AI Slop." Slop Mop distills Graphite's much broader research into nine tells: Posts stop users from scrolling by including one line of text and a gap that requires viewers to extend the post, use lots of hype words, contain no-catch promises, are written with flowery language, contain a suspiciously neat lesson, are engagement bait, rely on empty praise, define terms by contrast, and are worded stiffly. Those nine tells are what Slop Mop uses to identify potentially low-value writing, and two more questions are posed to prevent quality content from getting caught up in a Jevian purge: Whether what's written sounds like a particular person and whether it contains anything useful to readers. Both of those questions lower the score, Frazier explains on Slop Mop's website. A 12th question asks how likely it is that a model drafted the post, but that answer can only lower the slop score for writing that otherwise reads as human-written and cannot cause a post to be flagged on its own. None of those factors alone will distinguish AI from human writing, we note - as does Frazier. He describes Slop Mop less as an AI detector and more as a filter for eliminating internet garbage, no matter who or what writes it. “Slop Mop flags poor human content equally to poor AI content,” Frazier told The Register. “I feel that is the right decision because AI content can also be good and human content can also be bad.” The key question behind Slop Mop, Frazier writes in an FAQ on the extension’s website, is whether a post is worth your attention. Slop Mop will either flag or hide posts that meet its threshold for slop, whether AI- or human-written, meaning that they won’t actually be removed from a LinkedIn feed and can still be reported using the “Seems Like AI Slop” button. Rather than being frustrated by LinkedIn slop and looking for a way to stop seeing it, Frazier told us his primary motivation in building Slop Mop was to experiment with Jev, the new probabilistic decision AI model from TypeSafe that was released last week. Jev isn’t a chatbot and can’t be interacted with like one. Instead, it is designed to make probabilistic decisions for other software systems and is accessed via an API. It’s quickly become a hit on the internet, with developers finding lots of new applications for the cheap, fast model. Slop Mop, in other words, is another attempt by another developer to run Jev through its paces, and it's been open-sourced so others can do the same if they wish, and they might want to: The entire thing is running on Frazier’s Jev account, and thus is running requests through his API key, so if it proves to be a hit, he might be looking at some sizeable bills. Frazier has implemented daily post caps for users to prevent them from running up his bill. Up to 250 posts can be analyzed per installation per day, which he said well exceeds how most use LinkedIn. “From my early install base I am seeing a cost per user of ~$0.005 per day,” Frazier told us. “If this project goes viral, I might re-evaluate the free service portion of it but for now I am happy to eat those costs to understand tuning parameters and build the dataset.” If enough people find it useful and it doesn’t break the bank, Frazier also said he may extend its capabilities beyond LinkedIn to other platforms, but for now he’s treating it purely as a Jev experiment. Speaking of which, he’s pleased with the early results, saying that the project shows off Jev’s capabilities as “a new class of AI infrastructure that is here to stay.” “The context aware decision making with a confidence score will do more for enterprise workflows than LLMs have to date,” Frazier predicts. You can grab your own copy of Slop Mop on GitHub, or install it from the Chrome Web Store. ® Jev-powered Slop Mop demonstrates another great use case for probabilistic, non-chatty AI model Bull, the system builder behind JUPITER, takes delivery of SiPearl's long-awaited Rhea1 CPUs PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity Good news: there's a patch.
