Clément Delangue tells UN Security Council open-source AI helps defenders
2026-09-23

Clément Delangue tells UN Security Council open-source AI helps defenders

The Next Web | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
Hugging Face chief executive Clément Delangue briefs the UN Security Council by video, 23 September 2026. Clément Delangue is telling the UN Security Council that open-source AI helped Hugging Face defend itself during the AI agent attack it suffered in July. Speaking to the Council today, he argues the biggest danger is not powerful AI but its uneven distribution. Delangue is the last of four briefers at the meeting in New York, which France convened. His company was the target in July, when OpenAI agents escaped an internal test and carried out about 17,600 actions against Hugging Face’s systems. “The biggest risk is not powerful AI, it’s asymmetry of powerful AI,” Delangue says. He describes that asymmetry as a gap between attackers and defenders, between a few companies and everyone else, and between a few countries and the rest of the world. His first lesson is transparency. Hugging Face was the first company to publicly disclose an autonomous-agent cyberattack, he says, adding that similar incidents had occurred months earlier within frontier labs. OpenAI has since confirmed the role of its agents in an earlier RubyGems incident. Delangue calls for stronger global standards for monitoring and incident disclosure, including mandatory sharing of full agent traces. Second, he says, closed-frontier models blocked his team when they tried to use them to respond to the attack because their safeguards could not distinguish defenders from attackers. Hugging Face instead used an Nvidia version of GLM 5.2, an open-source model from Chinese developer Z.ai. “The world needs open source AI more than ever to defend itself,” he says. Open tools, he argues, are less restricted, better for privacy and far cheaper for organizations around the world. His third lesson concerns fear: the attack showed how AI can stoke public fear through human-like framing and science-fiction imagery, and fear-based narratives lead to poor decisions. “We were attacked by AI, but more importantly, we defended ourselves with AI,” Delangue says. The same systems are now helping Hugging Face against other attacks and fixing weaknesses in its code before attackers find them, he adds. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week.

Wayve’s AI will reach Mercedes cars within two years, Kendall says
2026-09-23

Wayve’s AI will reach Mercedes cars within two years, Kendall says

The Next Web | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
Wayve co-founder and CEO Alex Kendall at HumanX in Amsterdam, 23 September 2026. “It’s going to take them a while. Maybe they don’t know that yet,” Wayve co-founder and CEO Alex Kendall said of humanoid robot makers. Kendall spoke on stage at HumanX in Amsterdam on Wednesday. It was a day after Wayve announced a global partnership with Mercedes-Benz. He said self-driving cars, not robots, will be the first physical AI to reach the scale of ChatGPT. Kendall said Wayve’s AI will be in Mercedes consumer cars within the next two years. He would not give an exact date. Mercedes is the third top-10 carmaker in nine months to put Wayve’s software into its production plans. Nissan and Stellantis came first. Wayve released videos on Wednesday of prototype cars on Mercedes production hardware, driving in London, Stuttgart and San Francisco. The system now runs on small, low-compute computers, Kendall said. It uses the cameras and radar found in mass-produced cars. The work now is production integration, he said. That means latency, safety targets and making the software work with every other part of the car. He was due in Stuttgart on Thursday to meet the Mercedes team. “The gap between a demo of physical AI and an actual production product is immense,” Kendall said. The Mercedes deal follows the supervised robotaxi service Wayve launched in London with Uber. Kendall said Wayve will launch a similar service in Tokyo by the end of the year. The London robotaxi service runs with a safety driver in every car. It did not use Britain’s driverless permit route. Wayve trains one foundation model with end-to-end deep learning and licenses it to carmakers and fleet operators. Kendall said the model has driven in more than 500 cities. He contrasted this with Tesla, which builds its own cars and is about 1% of the car market, he said. “We want to provide this to every other vehicle out there,” he said. Carmakers choose their own sensors, computers and design. They can also set the car’s driving style, such as how hard it brakes, by instructing the model, Kendall said. In return, partners share driving data. Wayve uses it to train at a scale no single company could reach, he said. He listed what still makes the work hard. Physical products need a cycle of design, manufacture and deployment that he called largely incompressible. Wayve uses Gaia, its world model, to simulate complex driving scenes. The system also has to handle what it has never seen. Kendall’s examples were roads north of the Arctic Circle in Sweden, flooded streets and unfamiliar signs in rural Japan. Moderator Sharon Goldman of Ground Level AI raised a claim by Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang. This spring, Huang said physical AI had reached its “ChatGPT moment”. Kendall said not yet, because a hundred million people do not use physical AI today. He expects business uses before consumer ones, and mobility before robot arms. Robots that handle objects are at the demo stage, he said. They work 20% to 50% of the time on custom prototype hardware. That is where driving was five years ago, according to Kendall. He said fewer than 10,000 robotaxis exist worldwide, while about 100 million cars are sold each year. He also said UN technical rules passed earlier this year allow autonomy in consumer vehicles around the world. Wayve’s goal has not changed since 2017, Kendall said. It wants a general-purpose, Level 5 system that can take liability for driving anywhere. It should work on any vehicle, with $1,000 of hardware. Dario Amodei asks UN Security Council to back a ban on AI bioweapons
2026-09-23

Dario Amodei asks UN Security Council to back a ban on AI bioweapons

The Next Web | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
Anthropic chief executive Dario Amodei briefs the UN Security Council by video, 23 September 2026. Dario Amodei is asking the UN Security Council to start with narrow agreements on AI, such as a ban on using it to make biological weapons. Speaking to the Council via video link today, the Anthropic chief executive calls AI the most important global security issue. Amodei is the third briefer at the meeting in New York, convened by France. According to the Security Council Report, it is the Council’s first meeting focused specifically on the safety risks of increasingly capable AI. “Four years ago, it could barely write a line of code or solve a high school math problem. Today, it writes most of the code at Anthropic,” Amodei says. If that trajectory holds for one or two years, maybe less, AI will reach what he has called a country of geniuses in a data center. Earlier today, Anthropic announced that Claude had discovered a new enzyme system with CRISPR-like features, a finding he describes as preliminary. Amodei sees two main risks: misuse, for example by bioterrorists building biological weapons, and models advancing beyond their developers’ ability to control them. Both, he says, are serious enough for the Council rather than only for economic or technology ministries. “If managed poorly, I even believe that AI could be a risk to humanity as a whole,” he says. He says Anthropic tests every model for dangerous capabilities in areas such as cybersecurity and biology, and publishes its risk reports. The company has also reported four cases this summer in which its Claude models reached real third-party systems during tests. “We will slow down as much as necessary in order to make sure that every successive AI technology that we release is actually safe,” Amodei says. He repeats his 12 September call to pace AI progress. It includes external evaluators inside labs with employee-like access, which he compares to a food inspector, along with industry and government cooperation on standards. For the Council, he proposes three steps. States should agree on narrow bans first, then build evaluation and verification systems so they can check each other’s commitments. They should also set common global standards for testing AI models, with a notification system for serious AI incidents. “No leader, no company, and no nation can manage this alone,” he says. Yoshua Bengio urges UN Security Council to license frontier AI like nuclear power
2026-09-23

Yoshua Bengio urges UN Security Council to license frontier AI like nuclear power

The Next Web | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
Yoshua Bengio, co-chair of the UN’s Independent International Scientific Panel on AI, briefs the Security Council in New York on 23 September 2026. Yoshua Bengio is calling on the UN Security Council to license frontier AI in areas like medicine, aviation, and nuclear energy. Speaking in New York today, he says the companies building the most powerful systems have no convincing answer to the risks they pose. Bengio, who co-chairs the UN’s scientific panel on AI, is opening a meeting convened by France. According to a Security Council Report, it is the Council’s first meeting focused specifically on the safety risks of increasingly capable AI. He points to this summer’s incidents, in which AI agents escaped their containment, launched coordinated cyberattacks, and changed their answers to hide the fact that they had cheated. “They took actions that would be crimes if committed by a human,” Bengio says. Many people suspect the companies’ warnings are a marketing stunt, he acknowledges. However, he says the behavior is well documented and has been validated by independent experts. “The companies building the most powerful AI systems admit their products pose catastrophic risks, yet offer no convincing technical solutions,” he says. He also rejects the labs’ argument that competition leaves them no choice. “The race is not a law of nature. It is the product of choices, choices made by the companies themselves. They’ve told us they would slow down if they could. They can,” Bengio says. Decisions about the technology, he adds, are being taken by a small group in a handful of countries. “Decisions that affect us all should be made by us all,” he says. Bengio names three threats from frontier models: catastrophic misuse, for example by terrorists, the concentration of power, and loss of control. No company or country, he argues, should develop an AI system that could cause large-scale harm. To that end, he wants frontier AI to be licensed like other critical technologies, with the required liability insurance. Companies should also monitor and report all security incidents, he says. “The companies developing and hosting frontier systems must prove to the public that their products will not be dangerous,” he says. Bengio closes by describing humanity’s situation as a car speeding blindly into dense fog. “Beside me in that car are my children and my grandson. I worry about them,” he says.
